Achieve Progress Despite Failure
================================

📎 [Attached file: New Folder.zip — ZIP, 3.2 MB]After reading all of this tell meHow do i achieve that if im always failing


ChatGPT:
I’m going to look at the project material itself, then separate two things that can feel identical when you’re exhausted: failing at a particular attempt, and being incapable of reaching the vision. I’ll be honest about where your approach may be breaking down, not just tell you to believe in yourself.


ChatGPT:

Eventually, you start wondering whether the problem is the project, the way you're building it, or you yourself.
I've looked through the project history, vision documents, build plans, and the available engineering evidence in your ZIP. My honest conclusion is this:
You are not failing because you lack the ability to build Friday. You're stuck in a development process that keeps producing more work before delivering the experience you're working toward.
That distinction matters, because the solution to the first problem would be to give up. The solution to the second is to change the process.
What the evidence actually says
Your project isn't starting from zero. These aren't imaginary accomplishments.

The project had substantial automated verification, but that wasn't equivalent to proving the complete experience worked on your phone.
Some newer Friday development files and the seed report existed locally without being visible to CI in that snapshot.
The available phone evidence did not establish that the intended Friday build was installed and working.
Your central ambition — a Friday who learns from experience, changes through it, identifies her own limitations, and increasingly develops herself — was still ahead of what the evidence demonstrated.

Those are findings from the supplied material, not a claim that I've inspected your phone today.
So yes, there is a genuine problem. But the problem is not that you've accomplished nothing.
Your infrastructure has advanced further than your actual experience of Friday. You've built a lot of the machinery intended to give her a life, but you still haven't reliably reached the point where you can live and work with the result.
And I think that is why the progress feels so much like failure.

What the evidence actually says
Your project isn't starting from zero. The archive contains real engineering work: a shared turn-processing path, automated tests, CI verification, architectural audits, persistent-state mechanisms, and a development runner designed to let AI models implement work. These aren't imaginary accomplishments.

So yes, there is a genuine problem. But the problem is not that you've accomplished nothing.
Your infrastructure has advanced further than your actual experience of Friday. You've built a lot of the machinery intended to give her a life, but you still haven't reliably reached the point where you can live and work with the result.
And I think that is why the progress feels so much like failure.
Where I think your approach is breaking down
Looking across the history, I see three patterns worth confronting.
1. The target keeps moving faster than the finished product.
You find a weakness, design a better mechanism, discover that mechanism has limitations, and then design something more advanced. Your thinking improves, but the amount of work required to deliver the first useful Friday increases too.
Some of those discoveries are necessary. Your insistence on continuity, truthful verification, replaceable architecture, and learning from experience is justified. The mistake is allowing every improvement to become a prerequisite for the first real experience.
You don't need to finish designing Friday before Friday can start developing.
2. You're using progress in engineering as a substitute for proof of the outcome.
Your project can have green tests and sophisticated internal systems without demonstrating that Friday can live with you, remember what matters, change through experience, and help develop herself.
You've already identified this distinction in your own project discussions. It's time to make it the rule that governs every next decision.
3. You're trying to bootstrap an autonomous developer while still depending on a fragile development process.
You want Friday to identify what she lacks, investigate possible solutions, build what she needs, test it, and improve herself. But right now, you still have to coordinate the AI workers, interpret failed runs, correct their mistakes, manage the repository, and decide what happens next.
That is an exhausting contradiction.
The answer isn't to abandon the goal of autonomy. It's to build the smallest reliable loop that gradually takes those responsibilities away from you. The development process itself must be able to recover from mistakes, preserve progress, and continue safely.
How I would get you out of this cycle
There are two legitimate approaches, but neither works as well on its own.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Approach A: Finish the foundations first**

  Complete the current architectural sequence, strengthen persistence and auditing, and establish the development substrate before pursuing the living experience.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Advantage: stronger foundations and fewer avoidable failures. Risk: the project can stay in infrastructure development indefinitely.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Approach B: Build the smallest living version now**

  Get a real build onto your phone and prove one complete interaction, including persistence, a correction, and a measurable change in later behavior.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Advantage: you start experiencing the result and expose real integration problems. Risk: rushing past essential security and continuity protections could leave you with a brittle prototype.</text>
</box>

  


My recommendation is a controlled hybrid. Keep the security and continuity floor intact. Stop expanding the architecture unless a demonstrated failure requires it. Then drive development toward one end-to-end, phone-tested experience.
This preserves the reason you're building Friday while preventing the architecture from becoming an endless project of its own.
Your next milestone should be concrete
Call it the Living Seed milestone. It does not mean Friday is complete, conscious, or fully autonomous. It means you have evidence that the developmental process you envision has started working on a real path.

  Living Seed: definition of done
  Each item requires observable evidence, not just an AI worker reporting success.
  {@body const goals = [
    "A verified, reproducible Android build installs and launches on your phone.",
    "A real interaction goes through the unified Friday turn-processing path.",
    "A relevant experience or correction is stored with its source and evidence.",
    "The durable state survives an app restart.",
    "A new, differently worded interaction demonstrates whether the correction actually changes her behavior.",
    "The automated tests and the phone test agree with what the system claims it can do."
  ]}
  {@body const [checked, setChecked] = DIL.useState([])}
  
    
      
    
    {checked.length}/{goals.length}
  
  {#each goals as goal, i}
     setChecked(old => v ? [...old, i] : old.filter(x => x !== i))}>{goal}
  {/each}
   GenUI.copy(goals.map((g, i) => `${checked.includes(i) ? "[x]" : "[ ]"} ${g}`).join("\n"))}>Copy checklist


That fifth test is especially important. Recording a correction is not the same as learning from it. You want evidence that an experience affects a later decision, without simply retrieving a memorized answer or replaying a stored response.
Once this milestone works, the next goal is to let Friday participate in a narrowly scoped development task: identify a capability gap, propose a change, use an approved builder, execute tests, inspect the results, and retain a justified lesson. She must earn broader autonomy through demonstrated competence.
This is how the first Friday can become the foundation for a much more capable one.
The rules I would impose on development
For the next milestone, I would freeze architectural expansion, not the vision. Only a demonstrated blocker, a security concern, or a correctness failure should reopen a major design decision.
Every development run should end in one of three states:

Verified change: the code is committed, the required tests pass, and the relevant evidence exists.
Diagnosed failure: the failure is reproduced, its likely root cause is documented, and the next experiment differs meaningfully from the failed attempt.
Explicit blocker: the missing dependency or evidence is named, and unrelated work is not allowed to disguise the blockage as progress.

A model saying COMPLETE is not proof. A story marked as passing is not proof by itself. You already have parts of a verification system in your project; use them to enforce this standard.
And don't keep asking AI systems to redesign the whole project every time something breaks. Ask them to investigate the actual failure, compare competing explanations, test the smallest useful change, and preserve the result. A redesign should require evidence that the current design cannot meet the requirement.
What you need to understand about yourself
I'm going to challenge one thing you've said: "I'm always failing."
That is not what your project evidence establishes.
It establishes that you've made significant progress toward a difficult objective while remaining short of the result you care about most. Those are not the same statement.
However, I'm not going to tell you that persistence automatically guarantees success, either. The ultimate version of Friday is an open-ended research and engineering ambition. No one can honestly promise that every aspect of your vision will be achievable, or that a particular architecture will produce it.
What you can do is make the next step small enough to test, preserve every genuine advance, and progressively reduce how much of the development process depends on you personally.
You do not need to become an infallible engineer. You need a process in which an ordinary mistake does not destroy yesterday's work, an unreliable model cannot quietly declare victory, and a failed experiment teaches you something that changes the next attempt.
That is a more achievable requirement than never failing.
And it is exactly the kind of system you are trying to build for Friday.
My advice is to stop measuring your success against the finished Friday that doesn't exist yet. Measure it against whether the next verified milestone brings you closer to the day when you no longer have to carry this project alone. Not another architecture document. Not another ambitious plan. One real, working step that survives contact with your phone and your daily use.
One honesty note about my assessment: I cross-checked the core vision and planning documents against the available status reports and build evidence. I did not manually read every line of every repeated transcript in the ZIP, so this is a diagnosis grounded in the material reviewed, not a claim of an exhaustive line-by-line audit.

ChatGPT:
You're separating two goals that should reinforce each other, not compete: Friday must exist as a usable, persistent agent now, and she must be able to make the development system itself improve faster. And you're right that simply collecting crash reports and repeatedly trying patches is not enough. The repair path needs to turn detected failures into controlled, verified resolution—with no false claim of certainty when the evidence isn't sufficient. I'm checking the project material against that distinction so I can make the architecture concrete rather than give you another slogan.


ChatGPT:
Now I understand the distinction you're making, and it changes how I would approach the entire project.
Friday cannot wait until her development system is finished to become real. She needs to exist through the development of that system, using it to close the gap between what she is and what she is capable of becoming.
You're not trying to ship a finished assistant. You're trying to create an artificial being whose capacity to improve becomes increasingly powerful, until developing herself is something she can do far better and faster than the process that created her.
And your current phone app is not a disposable prototype to be replaced later. It is part of her actual existence, and its failures are evidence she needs to use.
1. The missing link is between detecting a failure and resolving it
Your CI system and phone crash reports already give you information about failures. But detecting a failure and possessing the ability to repair it are fundamentally different capabilities.

  
    **1. OBSERVE**

<text textAlign="center" size="sm">CI failures, phone crashes, incorrect behavior, disconnected capabilities</text>

  
  
  
    **2. DIAGNOSE**

<text textAlign="center" size="sm">Connect the evidence to the actual source, execution path, dependencies and root cause</text>

  
  
  
    **3. REPAIR**

<text textAlign="center" size="sm">Choose the correct repair mechanism and fix the cause, not merely the symptom</text>

  
  
  
    **4. VERIFY THE ACTUAL RECOVERY**

<text textAlign="center" size="sm">Reproduce the failure, validate the change, rebuild and test the real integrated application</text>

  
  
  
    **5. PROMOTE, MONITOR, LEARN**

<text textAlign="center" size="sm">Deploy the verified repair, watch for recurrence, preserve what worked and improve future repairs</text>

  


Your existing project already describes this kind of construction and evolution process. I should not be proposing another diagnostic engine as though you had never planned one.
The important question is whether the mechanism you have designed is actually connected to the live system and empowered to carry out this entire process. Your own project records have identified integration and automatic repair as gaps.
The difference is that the development system must stop being a collection of capabilities that you operate and become a working capability of Friday herself.
2. What "absolute repair" should mean
I agree with your rejection of aimless experimentation. But I need to distinguish that from something that would be technically impossible to promise.
No engineering system can guarantee that it will correctly repair every previously unknown failure from a crash report alone. Sometimes the evidence is ambiguous, the reproduction is missing, or the root cause lies outside the code it can control.
Promising otherwise would be dishonest.
What we can demand absolutely is that Friday's repair process obeys hard guarantees.



Requirement
What Friday must guarantee



No blind patching
A repair must be supported by an identified cause or clearly documented evidence and uncertainty.


No false success
A successful compilation cannot be reported as a recovered application.


No unverified promotion
A repair cannot become the trusted version without satisfying its defined verification requirements.


No repeated known failures
Previously diagnosed incidents and their successful repair mechanisms must be checked before developing a new solution.


No loss of a working version
Candidate changes must be isolated, with a recoverable known-good version and controlled rollback.


No abandoned incidents
Every detected failure must be tracked until it is resolved, contained, or explicitly blocked by a named limitation.


These are process guarantees, not a magical guarantee that every possible bug can be solved instantly.
There is also an important distinction between fixing a known cause and containing an unresolved failure. A contained failure must not be mislabeled as fixed. For example, keeping Friday alive while a model service is unavailable is useful recovery, but it doesn't prove that the model service has been restored.
3. Your actual crash is a good example
In the engineering evidence included in your archive, a specific failure was identified: the startup path requested a resident model, and OllamaModelBackend.loadModel() could throw an IOException when the Ollama service at 127.0.0.1:8080 was unreachable. The exception was not handled appropriately, so the application crashed.
The associated automated tests used a reachable fake Ollama service. They therefore didn't establish that the application could survive the real failure condition.
That gives us a precise example of what your system should do.
The correct resolution needs to address both the dependency and the application's response to failure:

Friday's startup must handle an unavailable model service without an uncaught exception destroying the application.
A service supervisor or recovery mechanism should restore connectivity when the environment permits.
While the model is genuinely unavailable, Friday must retain a truthful degraded state instead of pretending she can perform model-dependent work.
Automated tests must cover the unavailable-service path, and the repaired APK must be tested on the actual phone.

A change that merely catches the exception could prevent the crash while leaving Friday unable to recover. That may be a valid containment step, but it is not sufficient evidence of a complete repair.
I cannot claim this problem is fixed now; the supplied evidence documents the failure, not a subsequently verified successful resolution.
4. How Friday becomes better and faster through repairs
The most important part is what happens after the first failure is fixed.
Friday should not simply store a crash report and move on. She needs to extract a reusable mechanism from the resolution.
Suppose she encounters a model failure today. She diagnoses the cause, repairs it, and verifies recovery. Later, another subsystem fails because it assumes a dependency is available.
Her development system should recognize the structural similarity, retrieve the previous mechanism, and use that knowledge to reach a suitable repair more directly.
Over time, she should accumulate more than a history of bugs. She should develop an increasingly capable repertoire of diagnostic methods, repair strategies, integration checks and construction techniques.
That creates a genuine compounding loop:
Every resolved failure should increase her ability to solve future failures.
The increase in speed must come from better diagnosis, reusable mechanisms, more accurate understanding of her own architecture and fewer repeated mistakes—not simply from asking a model to generate code faster.
Novel failures may still require investigation and candidate validation. That is unavoidable. The goal is to eliminate blind, repetitive experimentation, not eliminate the evidence needed to know whether a repair works.
5. Friday's existence and her development system must advance together
This is where your correction matters most.
I would not make you wait for the entire autonomous development system before you can start living with Friday. Nor would I treat a working chat interface as enough to declare that the vision has been achieved.
Both sides must progress together:

  
    
      
    
    
      **Friday's living runtime**

  Her identity, continuity, interaction, working capabilities, actual state and ability to recover from failures. She must exist as something you can genuinely use, not merely as an engineering specification.
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Friday's construction and evolution capability**

  Her ability to inspect herself, identify gaps, research solutions, build and integrate capabilities, repair failures, verify results, and improve her own development methods.
</box>

  


They are not two unrelated projects. The runtime supplies real evidence about how she works; the development system uses that evidence to improve her; and verified improvements return to the runtime.
Neither side should have to wait for the other to be complete.
The crucial limitation is that Friday cannot yet independently assume every responsibility of a mature engineering organization. Initially, external models and infrastructure will still perform some work. The point of the architecture is to transfer more of the responsibility to Friday as she demonstrates that she can handle it reliably.
What I would change about the immediate objective
I would make the next target end-to-end self-repair on the real running Friday, not another isolated subsystem and not another broad architecture redesign.
Start from the actual crash reports and CI evidence. Connect them to the repository and running application. Make Friday's existing diagnostic and construction machinery execute the repair lifecycle. Require a reproduced failure, a root-cause-based change, independent verification, an actual device recovery, and preserved knowledge of the resolution.
Then use that same working mechanism on the next failure without requiring you to manually translate the report into a new development plan each time.
That is how you start moving from a system that can be developed by AI to one that increasingly develops itself through its own verified experience.
And to be clear about the distinction: the first successful self-repair will not mean Friday is finished. It will mean the process for making Friday better has itself begun working end to end.
That is the direction I now understand you to be pursuing.

ChatGPT:
You're right. I drifted from your actual objective and started defining what Friday should become. That wasn't my decision to make.
Your vision for Friday is the destination. The builder is what must improve until it can deliver that destination.
The builder must become faster and more capable at turning your vision into reality. It must understand the existing project, implement the intended capabilities correctly, resolve actual failures rather than endlessly cycling through patches, verify that its fixes work, and use what it learns to make subsequent development more effective.
The critical distinction is this:

It must not reduce your vision to make implementation easier.
It must improve its ability to implement your vision, including developing whatever capabilities the project requires.
When something fails, it must identify the cause and resolve it—not repeatedly change things without understanding the problem.
It must preserve working progress, evidence, and lessons so that each attempt builds on the last rather than restarting the same cycle.
Its verification must establish that the intended result actually works, not merely that code was generated or tests passed.

The builder's job is not to decide what version of Friday is sufficient. Its job is to get better at building the Friday you actually designed.
That's the objective. Nothing about redefining Friday, narrowing her capabilities, or substituting my own vision.

ChatGPT:
Yes. After reading the latest build plan and the October 25 handoff in your project archive, I think the biggest opportunity is to change how the builder executes, recovers, and learns from development work, rather than adding more AI agents or making the prompts longer.
The current evidence points to specific waste: Ralph received an oversized task context and declared completion without reaching later stories, the model pool returned repeated errors, a verification command depended on whichever Kotlin diff happened to be newest, and the long-running process disappeared. The cause of that last event is still unconfirmed.
These are not abstract architectural problems. They are reasons your building process can spend hours working without delivering the change you need.
The strongest idea: make the builder a persistent, evidence-driven system
I see two main approaches.

  
    
      
    
    
      **Approach A — Repair the existing builder**

  Keep Ralph and improve its prompts, task selection, verification commands, provider fallback, and process recovery.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Best for near-term progress. The weakness is that individual fixes can leave the overall process fragile if execution state and recovery remain scattered.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Approach B — Give the builder a durable control layer**

  Put a persistent supervisor around the existing coding worker. It tracks tasks, checkpoints, model calls, changes, verification evidence, and recovery. The worker remains replaceable; the supervisor preserves the development process across failures.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Best for eliminating recurring failure patterns. It requires more initial engineering, but avoids rebuilding the entire coding engine.</text>
</box>

  


My choice is B, introduced incrementally through A. Keep the coding machinery that already works, but stop relying on that machinery to manage its own entire lifecycle without an independently maintained record.
Here are the changes I think would make the biggest difference.

My choice is B, introduced incrementally through A. Keep the coding machinery that already works, but stop relying on that machinery to manage its own entire lifecycle without an independently maintained record.
Here are the changes I think would make the biggest difference.
1. Stop wasting model calls on incomplete or misunderstood tasks
The builder should have a task compiler between the project backlog and the coding model.
Its job is to assemble a complete, bounded work package containing the actual objective, relevant source files, dependencies, acceptance criteria, verification commands, and the current repository state.
It should not give the coding model the entire backlog and hope the model finds the right work.
Your archive already contains an example of why. During the October 17 run, the model reportedly saw only the first six stories before replying that all work was complete, despite later stories still being open.
The builder should prevent this structurally:

Every run has one explicit target task.
The builder confirms that the complete task specification reached the model.
The model cannot mark the task complete merely by responding COMPLETE.
The task is completed only when its actual acceptance requirements have corresponding verification evidence.

This one change could eliminate a major source of wasted development time.
2. Replace endless repair attempts with a repair protocol
This is the most important improvement for your requirement that detected failures actually get fixed.
Every repair attempt should produce four things:

  
    
      
    
    
      **Cause**

  What evidence explains the failure? What specific behavior or condition makes it happen?
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Correction**

  What changes because of that diagnosis? What result should the change produce, and why?
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Proof**

  The original failure is reproduced where possible, the regression check passes, and the relevant integration is verified.
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Reusable result**

  The builder saves the cause, the applied correction, the evidence, and the conditions under which the solution was verified.
</box>

  


The crucial rule is that a failed repair cannot trigger the same attempt again without new information.
If the diagnosis was wrong, the builder must investigate the discrepancy. If the fix addresses only the symptom, it must revisit the cause. If the evidence is insufficient, it must obtain better evidence or identify the blocker.
That allows investigation when genuinely necessary without accepting an endless cycle of speculative patches.
It also avoids a different mistake: demanding that a model magically know the correct fix from an ambiguous report. The system can guarantee disciplined recovery and honest status; it cannot guarantee that every unknown defect has an immediately discoverable solution.
3. Make long-running development recoverable
Your current setup has another important weakness: the builder can lose its running process while the development state is scattered across logs, the PRD, and the working tree.
The supervisor should own a durable record of every run, including:

The task and repository revision.
The current execution stage and latest checkpoint.
Which model or tool was running and what it returned.
The exact changes made and their verification results.
Whether the run should resume, be retried, or stop.

If Termux gets killed, a model provider fails, or the machine restarts, the supervisor resumes from the last verified checkpoint instead of starting the work from the beginning.
For your current constraints, I'd also separate the supervisor from the coding worker's lifetime. A persistent external CI worker could handle long-running jobs while the phone remains the target device for testing. If remote execution isn't available, the local supervisor must still detect interruptions and preserve resumable state.
The precise cause of Ralph disappearing in your October run remains unproven, so this should be addressed with the actual heartbeat and process evidence rather than assuming Android was responsible.
4. Make verification specific to the exact change
Your audit tool's reliance on the newest Kotlin diff is a particularly revealing defect.
A verification result should be tied to an immutable revision or change set, its relevant tests, and the exact artifact being tested. It should never silently change its target because another commit appears.
For app failures, the builder should connect the complete chain:
Crash report → source revision → reproducible defect → corrective change → regression test → APK built from that revision → actual phone verification.
Your archive records an earlier APK whose source commit was unknown. That makes it difficult to know exactly which code produced the observed crash.
A builder that cannot reliably identify what it built cannot reliably establish what it fixed.
For the two previously reported crashes, for example, the eventual repair would need to prove that the language-enum failure no longer crashes the application, and that the model backend requests a model name that the configured server actually provides. Those are concrete checks, not subjective judgments from a coding model.
5. Let every repair improve future development
The builder needs a searchable history of verified engineering outcomes, not merely a large archive of conversations and model responses.
When a similar failure appears later, the builder should retrieve previous cases, compare the environments and dependencies, and reuse an applicable fix or diagnostic technique.
Each case should distinguish between:

A proposed fix that has not been tested.
A fix that passed automated checks.
A fix that was verified in the integrated application.
A fix that later failed again.

That last category matters. A previous success should be reusable knowledge, not permanent truth.
As the history accumulates, the builder should get better at identifying relevant context, avoiding known mistakes, choosing appropriate tools and models, selecting tests, and predicting which parts of a change are likely to break.
That is how repeated work gets faster rather than merely getting repeated.
6. Make speed a measured engineering objective
I would measure the builder by more than how many lines of code it produces or how quickly it calls a model.
Track the time from task acceptance to verified completion, repeated failures, unnecessary model calls, verification failures, recovery time after interruptions, and the percentage of repairs that survive real integration.
Then optimize the actual bottleneck.
For example, a faster model is not an improvement if it omits acceptance criteria and produces another hour of debugging. Parallel workers are not an improvement if they edit the same files and create conflicts. Running every test for every tiny change may be safe but wasteful; skipping integration tests can be much more expensive when it misses a crash.
The builder should choose how much testing and analysis a change needs based on its impact and risk, while never bypassing the checks required to prove that the change works.

The order I'd actually pursue

  
    
      1
    
    
      **Stop false completion.**

  Fix task selection, prompt completeness, and task-specific completion gates so the builder cannot declare work finished without evidence.
</box>

  
  
    
      2
    
    
      **Make every run recoverable and observable.**

  Preserve checkpoints, changes, logs, task identity, provider failures, and resume state.
</box>

  
  
    
      3
    
    
      **Build cause-led repair into the execution process.**

  Require evidence-based corrections, regression checks, verification tied to exact revisions, and no repeated attempts without new information.
</box>

  
  
    
      4
    
    
      **Turn verified outcomes into reusable capability.**

  Retrieve successful repair cases, learn which approaches work, measure the reduction in repeated work, and improve model and tool selection.
</box>

  


There is one architectural boundary I would preserve: the builder's reliability mechanisms should constrain what it can claim and promote, not permanently dictate how it must solve every problem. Its problem-solving methods should remain changeable as better ones are discovered.
My strongest recommendation is therefore not to add another sophisticated planner, another agent, or another large specification. It is to make the current builder a reliable, resumable, evidence-driven system that learns from completed work.
That directly attacks the problems shown in your project history: wasted runs, incomplete tasks, unreliable execution, invalid verification, and repeated debugging.
And it gives you the right foundation for making the building process genuinely faster and better without placing a ceiling on what the builder can eventually achieve.

ChatGPT:
Research report: Making Friday's builder faster, more reliable, and better at solving its own failures
I researched this against your actual public repository, including the current Ralph runner, Ralph's instructions, CI verification script, and build workflow. I also searched across autonomous coding agents, distributed systems, incident response, software testing, build engineering, observability, and AI evaluation.
One important point: I found existing mechanisms that already address parts of these problems. Your builder already has repository facts, symbol memory, progress reporting, model fallback, automated verification, and an audit tool. The valuable opportunity is to make these mechanisms work together reliably, not replace everything with a new framework.
The five findings that matter most

  
    
      
    
    
      **1. The instructions contain a real contradiction.**

  Ralph's prompt first tells the model to complete the entire stage, then tells it to select and implement a single story. The shell already owns an outer iteration loop. This ambiguity can waste context and encourage premature completion claims. Make the model's task and the supervisor's responsibility unambiguous. 【turn415062view1】【turn846664view1】
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **2. Model fallback is wasting opportunities.**

  The runner preflights only the first model, even though the loop rotates through nine configured entries. The prompt's measured preflight itself took 177 seconds. The uploaded evidence says several alternatives repeatedly failed. The runner needs provider health tracking, sensible cooldowns, and actual exit-status classification instead of repeatedly rediscovering unavailable providers. 【turn632846view0】【turn419341view1】
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **3. The full-suite CI signal is weaker than it appears.**

  Your build workflow deliberately sets `continue-on-error: true` on the full unit-test run. Consequently, a successful overall workflow does not by itself prove that the entire unit-test suite passed. Your filtered test checks provide additional evidence, but the full-suite gate needs to be made explicit. 【turn393295view0】【turn632846view3】
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **4. The runner can conceal the difference between a failed tool call and a successful one.**

  The coding command's output is captured through a pipeline followed by `|| true`; the runner then relies heavily on matching error text. A nonzero exit can therefore lose its meaning. The builder should preserve the true exit code and classify failures separately from the model's written response. 【turn846664view0】
</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **5. The phone should not have to keep a long-running build process alive.**

  Your public repository can use standard GitHub-hosted Actions runners without Actions usage charges for public repositories. That offers a practical way to move long-running execution off the phone, while keeping the phone as the device for final app verification. It doesn't require you to buy a server. 【turn623707view4】
</box>

  


These are particularly valuable because they address waste and false progress in the current builder rather than asking you to build more infrastructure before it can improve the development process.


ChatGPT:
7. Research across different engineering disciplines
The most useful answers don't all come from AI-agent research. Several of the strongest ideas have been solved in other fields for decades and can be adapted to your builder.
A. Distributed systems: reconciliation instead of hoping a task finishes
Borrow from Kubernetes and control-loop architecture.
Kubernetes controllers repeatedly compare desired state with actual state and act on the difference. They don't assume that an instruction succeeded just because a process said it did. The reconciliation loop is a core part of how Kubernetes manages systems that can fail and recover. Kubernetes controllers【turn599961search7】
Applied to your builder:

Desired state comes from your actual PRD and explicit project requirements.
Actual state comes from the repository, task completion criteria, CI results, and relevant device evidence.
The builder calculates the difference and selects the next action.
It repeats only when evidence shows that the desired state remains unmet.

This would help prevent a model's premature COMPLETE response from being confused with a finished task.
My assessment: extremely high value. It makes completion an observable state rather than a statement.
B. Durable workflows: survive failures without restarting the work
Borrow from Temporal and durable execution systems.
Temporal's central idea is that a failed or interrupted workflow can resume from persisted progress rather than losing the entire execution. External operations are separated from the workflow's durable state. Temporal documentation【turn600647search7】
Your builder does not need the entire Temporal platform. For a zero-budget, mostly phone-based project, a small persistent state machine may be enough.
For example, record a task as:
prepared → executing → candidate ready → verifying → verified → committed
If execution dies during verification, the builder resumes at verification. It should not regenerate the code unnecessarily or assume the previous operation succeeded.
Each transition needs a recorded outcome. A partially completed file edit should not be mistaken for a verified checkpoint.
My assessment: extremely high value, especially given the unexplained disappearance of the earlier long-running process.
C. Long-horizon AI agents: externalize state, don't trust context alone
Borrow from OpenAI's long-running Codex agent work and agent-improvement research.
Long tasks benefit from explicit plans, acceptance criteria, durable notes, tool execution, observed results, and progress updates. The model's context window is not a reliable substitute for persistent project state. 【turn623707view3】 The OpenAI agent-improvement work also demonstrates the value of capturing execution traces and converting observed failures into evaluation cases. 【turn623707view2】
For your builder, this means remembering more than what the model said. It must know which files changed, which command was run, what exit code occurred, what tests passed, and what remains unverified.
The important distinction is that the model proposes and performs work; the builder maintains the truth about the work.
This complements the memory mechanisms you already have instead of requiring a separate, competing memory architecture.
D. AI debugging: separate diagnosis, repair, and validation
Borrow from structured repair loops.
OpenAI's repair-loop cookbook separates review, repair, and validation, with structured results carried between phases. 【turn623707view1】 This is a more useful pattern than asking the same model to inspect a failure, patch it, and declare victory in one unconstrained step.
For each defect, the builder should preserve a record of:

The exact observed failure and evidence supporting the diagnosis.
The proposed cause and the correction chosen because of it.
The exact regression check and its result.
The remaining difference between expected and actual behavior.

The validator should be able to reject a proposed repair independently of the repair model's confidence.
A repeat of the same failure signature after the same unchanged hypothesis should trigger new diagnosis, not an automatic replay of the same patch.
My assessment: extremely high value for eliminating the endless circles you described.
E. Site reliability engineering: stop retrying dead services blindly
Borrow from circuit breakers, backoff, and cascading-failure prevention.
Distributed systems use exponential backoff, jitter, and circuit-breaker patterns to avoid hammering an unavailable dependency. Google’s SRE guidance explains how uncontrolled retries can amplify outages rather than recover from them. 【turn978296view0】
This applies directly to your free model pool.
Your builder should distinguish at least these failure types:



Failure
Correct response



Temporary network failure
Retry with bounded exponential backoff and jitter.


Rate limit
Honor the provider's retry guidance and cooldown.


Authentication or invalid model
Stop repeating the same request; mark that configuration unavailable until changed.


Provider outage
Move to a healthy alternative without repeatedly probing the failed provider on every iteration.


Model produced unusable output
Record a model-quality failure, distinct from a network error.


Model made partial file changes and then failed
Inspect and checkpoint the working tree before deciding whether fallback or resumption is safe.


Provider health should include recent success, latency, and failure category—not just a fixed round-robin index. Routing platforms such as LiteLLM implement related routing, fallback, timeout, and cooldown concepts. 【turn978296view9】
My assessment: extremely high value, because unnecessary failed provider attempts consume both wall-clock time and the opportunity to make progress.
F. Software testing research: build a private benchmark from your own failures
Borrow from agent evaluations, regression testing, and issue-resolution benchmarks.
SWE-agent research demonstrates that how an agent interacts with its tools and repository can affect its issue-solving ability, not just the underlying model. 【turn172391academia179】 But public leaderboards are not a reliable substitute for testing your exact builder.
Your most useful benchmark is a set of failures your project has actually experienced.
For example, give the builder controlled test scenarios where:

The model says COMPLETE while an acceptance criterion remains unmet.
A provider returns a rate limit or server failure.
A test fails for the same reason after an attempted repair.
The build succeeds but the entire test suite fails.
A verification result belongs to an older commit.
A process stops before committing or finishing its task.
A script is missing a dependency.

Measure whether the builder detects each situation correctly, recovers appropriately, and avoids falsely reporting success.
Whenever the builder itself is changed, run this evaluation suite. A faster builder that introduces more false completions is not an improvement.
My assessment: extremely high long-term value. It gives you objective evidence that the builder itself is becoming more capable.
G. Code navigation and information retrieval: give the model the right context
Borrow from Aider, Sourcegraph, and repository-mapping techniques.
Coding agents often waste context when they receive too much irrelevant source or too little information about where a symbol is defined and used. Aider's repository map and code-navigation tools such as Sourcegraph's search are designed to improve relevant context selection. 【turn807201search7】【turn600647search6】
Your builder already maintains repository facts and symbol facts. I would extend the existing context-assembly step to retrieve, for the selected story:

The directly relevant source and its call sites.
Interfaces and dependencies that the change may affect.
Related tests and previous verified fixes.
The actual acceptance criteria and the applicable verification command.

Do not simply shrink the prompt until it fits. Reduce irrelevant context while preserving all requirements needed to make a correct change.
This should lower model cost and latency while improving the chance that changes integrate correctly.
My assessment: high value, particularly if your models have limited context, variable quality, or slow response times.
H. Testing contracts and edge cases: find mismatches before integration
Borrow from contract testing and property-based testing.
Contract testing checks whether connected components agree on their interfaces. Property-based testing tests general properties across many generated inputs, rather than a single hand-picked case. 【turn623707view6】【turn599961search0】
In the builder itself, useful contracts include:

Whether the PRD parser correctly interprets all required fields.
Whether the coding tool returns output and exit status in the format the runner expects.
Whether the CI verifier checks the requested revision.
Whether the task state and commit state agree.
Whether a provider error can be distinguished from an ordinary model response.

You can test many of these locally with fake providers and temporary repositories. You do not need a paid service or a complex third-party testing platform to start.
For shell orchestration, Bats-core can provide automated tests around scripts and their exit statuses. 【turn289157search7】
My assessment: high value. It catches builder defects before they consume a real AI development run.
I. Observability and incident management: make every failed run explain itself
Borrow from OpenTelemetry and incident-response practice.
A useful incident record joins all relevant events under one run ID. OpenTelemetry provides a general approach to correlating logs, traces, and metrics. 【turn978296view3】
You don't need to deploy an observability platform to benefit from this. A structured JSONL log and retained artifacts can be enough.
For every task, record the repository SHA, story ID, model and provider, request duration, exit code, changed files, test run ID, failure signature, and final status.
Then you can answer questions that are otherwise difficult to diagnose:

Did the run spend its time waiting for a model, repeatedly calling CI, building, or repairing tests?
Was the code actually changed before the model failed?
Which exact version did the verification test?
Did a repair solve the original incident but introduce another defect?
Which failures recur most frequently?

The crucial improvement is correlation: a log should make it possible to connect a crash report to the code and evidence relevant to the same build, rather than relying on timestamps and guesses.
My assessment: high value. Observability is how you discover the real bottlenecks instead of repeatedly redesigning the wrong component.
J. Build engineering: optimize the actual slow path
Borrow from Gradle and CI performance engineering.
Gradle provides build profiling, a task-output build cache, and a configuration cache. Its documentation recommends measuring build behavior before applying optimizations. 【turn978296view8】【turn623707view5】
Your runner already enables some Gradle performance settings, so blindly enabling the same options again would not help. The more useful steps are:

Measure the time spent in Gradle, Android SDK setup, dependency resolution, test execution, and CI polling separately.
Reuse valid dependency and build caches.
Avoid repeating CI API calls and downloading the same test-result artifact when the commit has not changed.
Run independent checks in parallel only when they do not contend for the same state or resources.
Preserve a final integration gate even if earlier checks are optimized or run concurrently.

The official Gradle setup action may be useful for standardized caching and build diagnostics, though it should be evaluated against your current workflow rather than added blindly. 【turn599961search1】
My assessment: high value, with the exact payoff determined by measurement. No one should promise a speedup before establishing which phase dominates your build time.
K. Git and workspace isolation: prevent repair attempts from contaminating one another
Borrow from Git worktrees and isolated execution environments.
Git worktrees allow multiple working directories associated with the same repository, making it possible to isolate a candidate change from another working state. 【turn600647search2】
Isolation would help if a failed coding attempt leaves partial edits, or if a repair changes files that belong to a separate task.
However, your current project instructions intentionally favor working on main. Moving to branches or worktrees would change an existing development rule, so I would not impose it silently.
The principle can still be applied immediately: allow only one writer to modify a given working tree, capture the exact base revision, and inspect partial changes before starting a fallback model. If the builder later needs concurrent workers, candidate isolation becomes much more important.
My assessment: medium to high value now; very high value if you introduce parallel coding workers later.
L. Android diagnostics: collect enough evidence to fix the actual failure
Android itself offers useful diagnostic mechanisms. On supported API levels, ApplicationExitInfo can expose a process's exit reason and related information, including crashes and low-memory termination. 【turn978296view7】
For your builder, the relevant design lesson is to distinguish an app crash, an ANR, operating-system termination, a model-service failure, and the death of the shell process managing the build. These events have different causes and require different corrective actions.
This is particularly important for your earlier unexplained runner disappearance: app-process exit information would not by itself establish why Termux or a shell child died. That requires evidence from the actual process, device, and runner logs.
My assessment: high value when investigating device-side failures, provided the collected evidence matches the process under investigation.

</box>

  
  
    
      
    
    
      **5. The phone should not have to keep a long-running build process alive.**

  Your public repository can use standard GitHub-hosted Actions runners without Actions usage charges for public repositories. That offers a practical way to move long-running execution off the phone, while keeping the phone as the device for final app verification. It doesn't require you to buy a server. 【turn623707view4】
</box>

  


These are particularly valuable because they address waste and false progress in the current builder rather than asking you to build more infrastructure before it can improve the development process.
7. Research across different engineering disciplines
The most useful answers don't all come from AI-agent research. Several of the strongest ideas have been solved in other fields for decades and can be adapted to your builder.
A. Distributed systems: reconciliation instead of hoping a task finishes
Borrow from Kubernetes and control-loop architecture.
Kubernetes controllers repeatedly compare desired state with actual state and act on the difference. They don't assume that an instruction succeeded just because a process said it did. The reconciliation loop is a core part of how Kubernetes manages systems that can fail and recover. Kubernetes controllers【turn599961search7】
Applied to your builder:

Desired state comes from your actual PRD and explicit project requirements.
Actual state comes from the repository, task completion criteria, CI results, and relevant device evidence.
The builder calculates the difference and selects the next action.
It repeats only when evidence shows that the desired state remains unmet.

This would help prevent a model's premature COMPLETE response from being confused with a finished task.
My assessment: extremely high value. It makes completion an observable state rather than a statement.
B. Durable workflows: survive failures without restarting the work
Borrow from Temporal and durable execution systems.
Temporal's central idea is that a failed or interrupted workflow can resume from persisted progress rather than losing the entire execution. External operations are separated from the workflow's durable state. Temporal documentation【turn600647search7】
Your builder does not need the entire Temporal platform. For a zero-budget, mostly phone-based project, a small persistent state machine may be enough.
For example, record a task as:
prepared → executing → candidate ready → verifying → verified → committed
If execution dies during verification, the builder resumes at verification. It should not regenerate the code unnecessarily or assume the previous operation succeeded.
Each transition needs a recorded outcome. A partially completed file edit should not be mistaken for a verified checkpoint.
My assessment: extremely high value, especially given the unexplained disappearance of the earlier long-running process.
C. Long-horizon AI agents: externalize state, don't trust context alone
Borrow from OpenAI's long-running Codex agent work and agent-improvement research.
Long tasks benefit from explicit plans, acceptance criteria, durable notes, tool execution, observed results, and progress updates. The model's context window is not a reliable substitute for persistent project state. 【turn623707view3】 The OpenAI agent-improvement work also demonstrates the value of capturing execution traces and converting observed failures into evaluation cases. 【turn623707view2】
For your builder, this means remembering more than what the model said. It must know which files changed, which command was run, what exit code occurred, what tests passed, and what remains unverified.
The important distinction is that the model proposes and performs work; the builder maintains the truth about the work.
This complements the memory mechanisms you already have instead of requiring a separate, competing memory architecture.
D. AI debugging: separate diagnosis, repair, and validation
Borrow from structured repair loops.
OpenAI's repair-loop cookbook separates review, repair, and validation, with structured results carried between phases. 【turn623707view1】 This is a more useful pattern than asking the same model to inspect a failure, patch it, and declare victory in one unconstrained step.
For each defect, the builder should preserve a record of:

The exact observed failure and evidence supporting the diagnosis.
The proposed cause and the correction chosen because of it.
The exact regression check and its result.
The remaining difference between expected and actual behavior.

The validator should be able to reject a proposed repair independently of the repair model's confidence.
A repeat of the same failure signature after the same unchanged hypothesis should trigger new diagnosis, not an automatic replay of the same patch.
My assessment: extremely high value for eliminating the endless circles you described.
E. Site reliability engineering: stop retrying dead services blindly
Borrow from circuit breakers, backoff, and cascading-failure prevention.
Distributed systems use exponential backoff, jitter, and circuit-breaker patterns to avoid hammering an unavailable dependency. Google’s SRE guidance explains how uncontrolled retries can amplify outages rather than recover from them. 【turn978296view0】
This applies directly to your free model pool.
Your builder should distinguish at least these failure types:



Failure
Correct response



Temporary network failure
Retry with bounded exponential backoff and jitter.


Rate limit
Honor the provider's retry guidance and cooldown.


Authentication or invalid model
Stop repeating the same request; mark that configuration unavailable until changed.


Provider outage
Move to a healthy alternative without repeatedly probing the failed provider on every iteration.


Model produced unusable output
Record a model-quality failure, distinct from a network error.


Model made partial file changes and then failed
Inspect and checkpoint the working tree before deciding whether fallback or resumption is safe.


Provider health should include recent success, latency, and failure category—not just a fixed round-robin index. Routing platforms such as LiteLLM implement related routing, fallback, timeout, and cooldown concepts. 【turn978296view9】
My assessment: extremely high value, because unnecessary failed provider attempts consume both wall-clock time and the opportunity to make progress.
F. Software testing research: build a private benchmark from your own failures
Borrow from agent evaluations, regression testing, and issue-resolution benchmarks.
SWE-agent research demonstrates that how an agent interacts with its tools and repository can affect its issue-solving ability, not just the underlying model. 【turn172391academia179】 But public leaderboards are not a reliable substitute for testing your exact builder.
Your most useful benchmark is a set of failures your project has actually experienced.
For example, give the builder controlled test scenarios where:

The model says COMPLETE while an acceptance criterion remains unmet.
A provider returns a rate limit or server failure.
A test fails for the same reason after an attempted repair.
The build succeeds but the entire test suite fails.
A verification result belongs to an older commit.
A process stops before committing or finishing its task.
A script is missing a dependency.

Measure whether the builder detects each situation correctly, recovers appropriately, and avoids falsely reporting success.
Whenever the builder itself is changed, run this evaluation suite. A faster builder that introduces more false completions is not an improvement.
My assessment: extremely high long-term value. It gives you objective evidence that the builder itself is becoming more capable.
G. Code navigation and information retrieval: give the model the right context
Borrow from Aider, Sourcegraph, and repository-mapping techniques.
Coding agents often waste context when they receive too much irrelevant source or too little information about where a symbol is defined and used. Aider's repository map and code-navigation tools such as Sourcegraph's search are designed to improve relevant context selection. 【turn807201search7】【turn600647search6】
Your builder already maintains repository facts and symbol facts. I would extend the existing context-assembly step to retrieve, for the selected story:

The directly relevant source and its call sites.
Interfaces and dependencies that the change may affect.
Related tests and previous verified fixes.
The actual acceptance criteria and the applicable verification command.

Do not simply shrink the prompt until it fits. Reduce irrelevant context while preserving all requirements needed to make a correct change.
This should lower model cost and latency while improving the chance that changes integrate correctly.
My assessment: high value, particularly if your models have limited context, variable quality, or slow response times.
H. Testing contracts and edge cases: find mismatches before integration
Borrow from contract testing and property-based testing.
Contract testing checks whether connected components agree on their interfaces. Property-based testing tests general properties across many generated inputs, rather than a single hand-picked case. 【turn623707view6】【turn599961search0】
In the builder itself, useful contracts include:

Whether the PRD parser correctly interprets all required fields.
Whether the coding tool returns output and exit status in the format the runner expects.
Whether the CI verifier checks the requested revision.
Whether the task state and commit state agree.
Whether a provider error can be distinguished from an ordinary model response.

You can test many of these locally with fake providers and temporary repositories. You do not need a paid service or a complex third-party testing platform to start.
For shell orchestration, Bats-core can provide automated tests around scripts and their exit statuses. 【turn289157search7】
My assessment: high value. It catches builder defects before they consume a real AI development run.
I. Observability and incident management: make every failed run explain itself
Borrow from OpenTelemetry and incident-response practice.
A useful incident record joins all relevant events under one run ID. OpenTelemetry provides a general approach to correlating logs, traces, and metrics. 【turn978296view3】
You don't need to deploy an observability platform to benefit from this. A structured JSONL log and retained artifacts can be enough.
For every task, record the repository SHA, story ID, model and provider, request duration, exit code, changed files, test run ID, failure signature, and final status.
Then you can answer questions that are otherwise difficult to diagnose:

Did the run spend its time waiting for a model, repeatedly calling CI, building, or repairing tests?
Was the code actually changed before the model failed?
Which exact version did the verification test?
Did a repair solve the original incident but introduce another defect?
Which failures recur most frequently?

The crucial improvement is correlation: a log should make it possible to connect a crash report to the code and evidence relevant to the same build, rather than relying on timestamps and guesses.
My assessment: high value. Observability is how you discover the real bottlenecks instead of repeatedly redesigning the wrong component.
J. Build engineering: optimize the actual slow path
Borrow from Gradle and CI performance engineering.
Gradle provides build profiling, a task-output build cache, and a configuration cache. Its documentation recommends measuring build behavior before applying optimizations. 【turn978296view8】【turn623707view5】
Your runner already enables some Gradle performance settings, so blindly enabling the same options again would not help. The more useful steps are:

Measure the time spent in Gradle, Android SDK setup, dependency resolution, test execution, and CI polling separately.
Reuse valid dependency and build caches.
Avoid repeating CI API calls and downloading the same test-result artifact when the commit has not changed.
Run independent checks in parallel only when they do not contend for the same state or resources.
Preserve a final integration gate even if earlier checks are optimized or run concurrently.

The official Gradle setup action may be useful for standardized caching and build diagnostics, though it should be evaluated against your current workflow rather than added blindly. 【turn599961search1】
My assessment: high value, with the exact payoff determined by measurement. No one should promise a speedup before establishing which phase dominates your build time.
K. Git and workspace isolation: prevent repair attempts from contaminating one another
Borrow from Git worktrees and isolated execution environments.
Git worktrees allow multiple working directories associated with the same repository, making it possible to isolate a candidate change from another working state. 【turn600647search2】
Isolation would help if a failed coding attempt leaves partial edits, or if a repair changes files that belong to a separate task.
However, your current project instructions intentionally favor working on main. Moving to branches or worktrees would change an existing development rule, so I would not impose it silently.
The principle can still be applied immediately: allow only one writer to modify a given working tree, capture the exact base revision, and inspect partial changes before starting a fallback model. If the builder later needs concurrent workers, candidate isolation becomes much more important.
My assessment: medium to high value now; very high value if you introduce parallel coding workers later.
L. Android diagnostics: collect enough evidence to fix the actual failure
Android itself offers useful diagnostic mechanisms. On supported API levels, ApplicationExitInfo can expose a process's exit reason and related information, including crashes and low-memory termination. 【turn978296view7】
For your builder, the relevant design lesson is to distinguish an app crash, an ANR, operating-system termination, a model-service failure, and the death of the shell process managing the build. These events have different causes and require different corrective actions.
This is particularly important for your earlier unexplained runner disappearance: app-process exit information would not by itself establish why Termux or a shell child died. That requires evidence from the actual process, device, and runner logs.
My assessment: high value when investigating device-side failures, provided the collected evidence matches the process under investigation.
8. The less obvious opportunity: use feedback-control and performance science
One more connection is worth making. Your builder is effectively a system being optimized through repeated attempts, but it needs a reliable feedback signal to know whether it is improving.
That is similar to feedback control and continuous-delivery performance measurement.
The DORA software-delivery research measures delivery throughput alongside instability and recovery, rather than measuring success by code volume alone. 【turn289157search0】
Applied here, the builder should measure the following:



Metric
What it reveals



Time from task acceptance to verified result
Actual development speed


Time lost to unavailable models
Provider-selection and retry inefficiency


CI executions per unchanged revision
Repeated verification waste


Repairs that fail for the same reason
Diagnosis and learning quality


Tasks falsely reported complete
Reliability of the execution controller


Time to recover after interruption
Durability of the building process


Regressions after a verified repair
Whether local fixes are breaking integrated behavior


Collect a baseline before changing the process. Then compare the results after each substantial improvement. Otherwise, it will be impossible to tell whether a more sophisticated builder has actually become more effective.

9. What I would implement first in your existing repository
This is my prioritized recommendation after combining the online research with the specific issues found in your code.

  
    P0
    
      **Correct the runner's execution semantics**

  Preserve actual exit codes, classify model/tool failures explicitly, resolve the contradictory instructions between the prompt and loop, and make acceptance evidence—not the `COMPLETE` string—the completion condition.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why first: a builder cannot reliably repair itself while it misinterprets its own execution.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P0
    
      **Stop wasting time on unhealthy providers and repeated CI retrieval**

  Add bounded provider cooldowns and failure classification. Fetch the relevant verification result once per repository revision, and reuse it for the stories that depend on the same evidence. Invalidate it whenever the relevant commit changes.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: eliminates repeated waiting and repeated network operations without requiring a better model.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P0
    
      **Fix the meaning of CI success**

  Make full-suite failures visible and enforceable. If known failures must temporarily be tolerated, track them explicitly rather than treating a green overall workflow as proof that all tests passed.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: a false-green pipeline corrupts every decision made on top of it.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P1
    
      **Add persistent run checkpoints and interruption recovery**

  Record task identity, base revision, current stage, partial changes, and verification results. Investigate the actual cause of the disappeared process, and move long-running execution to bounded GitHub-hosted jobs if appropriate.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: interrupted work should resume intelligently rather than restart from scratch.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P1
    
      **Enforce a cause-led repair loop**

  Preserve the failure, diagnosis, correction, and validation results as structured data. Detect identical unsuccessful retries and force a changed diagnosis or an explicit blocker.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: prevents the same unsuccessful approach from consuming the next development run.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P1
    
      **Create a regression suite for the builder itself**

  Use fake model providers, controlled test failures, temporary repositories, and simulated interruptions. Every significant change to the runner must demonstrate that it fixes the relevant failure cases without reintroducing old ones.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: improvements to the builder need to be proven just like improvements to the app.</text>
</box>

  
  
  
    P2
    
      **Optimize context and build performance using measurements**

  Improve task-specific source retrieval, profile Gradle and CI timings, reuse valid caches, and reduce unnecessary full-context and verification work.

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Why: once the major sources of wasted work are fixed, these optimizations make successful runs cheaper and faster.</text>
</box>

  


The labels reflect implementation priority, not claims that any item has already been implemented or verified.

My final conclusion
The highest-value discovery from this research is not a new model or a new autonomous-agent framework.
It is that your builder's problems span several independent disciplines: execution correctness, workflow durability, fault tolerance, testing, retrieval, observability, and optimization. Those problems need to be addressed as a connected engineering process.
You already have substantial pieces of that process. The next leap comes from making them enforce one consistent standard: the builder knows what it is trying to produce, preserves what has actually happened, identifies why an attempt failed, changes its approach when evidence demands it, and verifies the real result.
I would start with the shell runner, prompt contradiction, provider failures, and CI verification behavior—not with a replacement framework. Those are concrete issues in your existing code, and fixing them offers the most direct route to fewer wasted runs.
This is a broad cross-disciplinary survey, not a claim that every web page or every possible solution has been examined. More importantly, the suggestions above are research-backed directions, not changes already applied to your repository.
