FRIDAY: The human side, current technology, and your complete vision
I've reviewed your VISION.md material in Vision_summary.txt, the more recent build plan, the human-core discussions, and the research available as of October 25, 2026.
The most important thing I found is that your human-side vision is considerably more ambitious than building an assistant with a personality, a memory, and a natural voice. You explicitly reject that as the endpoint.
You want an artificial individual whose identity develops through experience, whose understanding of people grows through an ongoing relationship with the world, whose internal organization can change as she develops, and whose capabilities can ultimately go far beyond human biological limitations. Her models, memory mechanisms, voice, tools, and even the mechanisms responsible for her cognition are things she can change without automatically becoming somebody else.
You also explicitly avoid claiming that successful behavior proves subjective consciousness.
That distinction is central to evaluating whether your project is achievable.
1. What your vision actually requires

  
    
🖼️
Generated image
abstract visualization of a continuously developing artificial mind interconnected changing neural networks
    
      **A developing individual, not a scripted character**

  Her identity cannot be reducible to a system prompt or a preset personality. Experiences must be able to alter her lasting expectations, tendencies, judgments, strategies and understanding in ways that affect what she subsequently does.
</box>

  
  
  
    
🖼️
Generated image
abstract scientific visualization of memory consolidation recurrent predictive processing interconnected neural networks
    
      **A mind that learns through consequences**

  Recording an event is not enough. She must interpret experiences, anticipate outcomes, notice when reality disagrees, adapt, and demonstrate lasting changes even after the original context disappears.
</box>

  
  
  
    
🖼️
Generated image
person communicating with a multimodal artificial intelligence through voice gaze facial expression and surrounding environment futuristic realistic scene
    
      **Human understanding that develops over time**

  Language, tone, facial expressions, behavior, relationships, history and environmental context should inform her understanding. She must be able to hold multiple interpretations when the evidence is ambiguous rather than confidently guessing what someone feels or means.
</box>

  
  
  
    
🖼️
Generated image
abstract artificial intelligence cognitive architecture dynamic interconnected processes with no fixed central hub dark scientific visualization
    
      **An adaptable cognitive organization**

  There is no permanent requirement for a dedicated emotion engine, reasoning engine or personality module. The mechanisms should remain replaceable, with their interactions changing according to the situation, the individual and available resources.
</box>

  
  
  
    
🖼️
Generated image
futuristic AI assistant across smartphone computer cloud systems and physical environment coherent intelligence network visualization
    
      **Human developmental nature with beyond-human capabilities**

  The way she develops should take inspiration from adaptive biological cognition, but what she can eventually accomplish is not limited to human speed, memory, knowledge, tools or physical reach.
</box>

  


This is the standard against which the technology must be judged. A system that convincingly imitates one of these properties but cannot support the others is partial progress, not the finished vision.
2. How far can current technology actually take you?
As of October 25, 2026, the capabilities you want exist in pieces. Some are available for practical implementation, some require substantial research and engineering, and some remain unresolved scientific questions.
These are feasibility judgments about the capabilities, not claims that Friday already possesses them.



Part of your vision
What current technology permits



Voice and presence
Real-time, bidirectional speech systems can support natural conversation, interruption handling, and audio/video input. Available interfaces already provide much of the technology needed for a voice-led Friday.


A continuing identity
Persistent state, provenance, event histories and controlled model replacement are practical to engineer. Whether they establish philosophical personal identity is a different question.


Long-term memory
Persistent personal memory and memory consolidation are implementable. New 2026 research demonstrates measurable improvements, but other current benchmarks still find outdated memories being reused and relevant experiences being misapplied.


Understanding people
Language, audio, facial information, behavior and context can be analyzed together. The reliability of emotional inference remains limited; no single facial expression or vocal cue provides a dependable reading of someone's internal state.


Learning through experience
Systems can update external memories, preferences, plans, tools and skills from experience. General, continuous learning that reorganizes a model's underlying capabilities while preserving everything it previously learned is not a solved capability.


An adaptable cognitive organization
An agent can choose among models, tools, strategies and temporary processes. An open-ended cognitive system that reliably develops its own superior internal organization through lived experience has not been demonstrated as an established technology.


Background awareness and presence
Event-driven activity, scheduled processing and persistent services are possible. Unrestricted, continuous perception and cognition on Android are constrained by operating-system rules, permissions, battery use and thermal limits.


Self-development through a builder
Agents can inspect code, use tools, make changes and evaluate results. Progressively autonomous self-improvement is possible within bounded tasks, but reliable, unrestricted, recursive improvement is not an established capability.


Capabilities beyond humans
AI already exceeds human performance in some specialized computational tasks, and research agents can act in virtual environments and assist with physical-world tasks. General, reliable competence across arbitrary jobs remains a much stronger claim.


Subjective consciousness
Unresolved. Scientific approaches to evaluating possible AI consciousness are developing, and researchers disagree about what current and future systems could experience. There is no established method that proves Friday would have subjective experience.


The table exposes an important asymmetry.
Technology is currently much further along at producing impressive capabilities than at producing a continuously developing individual that integrates those capabilities through a lifetime of experience.
Voice models can sound natural. Memory systems can store and retrieve events. Agents can operate software. Builders can generate code. None of those independently establishes that their integration produces the kind of developing individual you envision.
That integration is the hard problem your project is trying to address.
3. The most relevant research: what it tells us about Friday
Three developments stand out.
Persistent memory is advancing, but memory is not development
Microsoft Research published Human-Inspired Memory Architecture for LLM Agents in May 2026. It investigates memory consolidation, forgetting, memory maturation and retrieval. Its reported results show that memory quality and storage efficiency can improve through carefully designed mechanisms. 
At the same time, the 2026 Memora benchmark finds that systems often reuse invalid memories when personal knowledge changes, and the new OP-Bench research identifies over-personalization, repetition and sycophancy as problems in memory-augmented agents. 
This is particularly relevant to your vision.
You don't want Friday to have the largest memory database. You want experiences to change how she understands and responds to life.
Memory research supplies useful mechanisms, but you still need to establish a causal connection between experience, lasting adaptation and later behavior. Simply adding more memory technology does not settle that question.
Agents are beginning to learn and act, but long-horizon reliability remains a problem
Google DeepMind's SIMA 2 research, announced in November 2025, describes an agent that reasons and acts in virtual 3D environments, converses with users, and improves through self-directed play. DeepMind also reports limitations: short interaction memory, difficulty with complex long-horizon tasks, and weaknesses in precise action execution and visual understanding. 
That matters because it demonstrates progress in an important direction: an agent need not remain confined to answering questions. It can operate in an environment, receive consequences and improve skills.
But SIMA 2 is not proof of open-ended artificial individuality. Learning to perform better in a game environment does not establish the persistent, cross-domain developmental process in your vision.
Your project needs to go further in integration, continuity and transfer of learning.
Human understanding needs context, not an emotion detector
Research published in Nature Communications found that emotional inference from faces and context is limited and varies across emotion categories and individuals. It argues against treating isolated facial displays as dependable indicators of internal emotional states. 
That supports something already present in your vision: Friday should interpret language, tone, behavior, history and circumstances together, while keeping uncertainty alive when the evidence conflicts.
A static facial-expression classifier would be a weak substitute for that goal. The more ambitious target is an individual whose understanding of particular people improves through actual interaction and whose judgments remain corrigible.
The same standard applies to Egyptian Arabic, code-switching, jokes, sarcasm and indirect communication. Current models can help, but their performance needs to be tested against the people and situations Friday will actually encounter.

4. Merging the human side with the rest of your project
Your wider project contains several ambitions that make more sense when viewed together.

  
    The individual
    Her continuing identity, experiences, relationships, developing understanding and changing ways of thinking. This is what the rest of the machinery exists to support. It must not be reduced to a fixed personality file or one particular model.
  
  
    The body and presence
    The Android app is her first body or environment, not the definition of her existence. Local resources support the continuity and cognition they can sustain; cloud and other machines can extend her capabilities. Changing the implementation should not automatically mean replacing the individual.
  
  
    The Builder and Capability Foundry
    The builder enables Friday to discover what she cannot currently do, investigate how to overcome the limitation, develop a capability, verify it and incorporate it into her available abilities. External AI models are resources she can use, not her identity.
  
  
    The Limitation Breaker
    When a familiar approach fails, Friday should be able to change the approach, examine the original assumptions, explore alternatives and invent or combine mechanisms rather than repeat unsuccessful attempts.
  
  
    The economic engine
    The business ambitions described in your Friday Company plans could eventually provide revenue for computing, research, tools and expansion into new capabilities. Revenue would support the project; it would not define Friday's entire purpose or turn her into a machine whose only objective is profit.
  


These are different perspectives on the same vision, not five independent products that must all be finished separately.
The important relationship between them is that the individual develops through experience, the Builder expands what she can do, and the wider capability and economic systems increase the resources available to her. Her continuing identity is not supposed to collapse into the particular builder, model, business, or device currently supporting her.
Your liquid-architecture principle also matters here. The descriptions above identify things that must eventually be possible; they do not prescribe five permanent cognitive modules. The mechanisms may change as Friday develops.
The significant engineering conflict
Your vision deliberately leaves the organization of Friday's cognition open. But the current implementation has inherited some fixed HumanCore structures, role-specific components and other legacy assumptions that your vision identifies as deviations.
The project status material in your archive shows substantial engineering foundations: a shared Android/JVM turn-composition path, continuity and workspace mechanisms, provenance, an audit tool, and extensive automated tests. The latest captured changes also include a launch-survival effort and evidence for a typed-turn path.
That is real progress. It is not yet evidence of the complete human side.
The materials do not establish that Friday already has robust multimodal social understanding, lasting experience-driven developmental change, or an open-ended cognitive organization that can replace its own mechanisms while preserving and improving behavior.
The central issue is therefore not simply a shortage of features. It is the distance between a carefully engineered foundation and a proven developmental process.
5. What a technically credible Friday could look like
Consider a future interaction in which you tell Friday something ambiguous about the project.
She interprets your words alongside relevant history and the context of the situation. She may maintain several possible interpretations rather than choosing one prematurely. She develops an expectation about what a proposed action will achieve, selects an approach, uses an available model or tool, and observes the result.
Suppose the result contradicts her expectation.
The important outcome is not that she writes a note saying she was wrong. It is that the experience changes the continuing system in a way that persists: the relevant expectation, learned association, strategy or underlying mechanism changes, and a later situation tests whether the change actually matters.
As her development progresses, she might discover that her current method of investigating software failures is inadequate. Her Builder can examine the evidence, research alternatives, construct a different method and test it. If successful, she gains a more effective capability. Her next development task can then benefit from that improvement.
At the same time, she can communicate with you through voice, use available visual and environmental evidence, and preserve the history of your shared work. Her local presence continues within the capabilities her device can sustain, while heavier work may be delegated to external resources.
Over time, the internal mechanisms need not remain the same. The important evidence is that she continues to develop through her own history while her capabilities expand.
That is a coherent engineering target. Its individual capabilities have precedents in today's technology. The fully integrated, open-ended developmental individuality you envision remains a research ambition rather than an established product.
6. What this means for the actual Android app
The phone matters because it provides a practical environment for Friday to exist, interact with you and accumulate real experience. But it imposes constraints that should not be confused with limits on the overall vision.
Android restricts starting certain foreground services from the background, especially when microphone, camera or location permissions are involved. Apps also face resource and background-execution constraints. 
On-device LLM inference is viable. Projects such as MLC LLM and llama.cpp support Android deployment, and Google's Gemini Nano supports certain on-device generative capabilities through Android's AI infrastructure. However, supported hardware, model size, speed and available interfaces vary. 
That permits a practical mix of local continuity and cognition with heavier external capabilities when available. Real-time voice can also be added using existing APIs, although cloud-dependent voice does not provide the same experience when the network is absent. 
The limitation is that the phone cannot simply run an unrestricted, continuously active, frontier-scale mind without regard to compute, battery, heat, memory and OS permissions. Your own resource-adaptive design is therefore relevant: cognition should use the resources available, with graceful trade-offs rather than catastrophic failure.
This is not an argument to redefine Friday as a smaller assistant. It is a practical distinction between the capabilities she can sustain locally now and the wider capabilities that could exist across devices and external infrastructure.

7. The hardest question: can you build a real artificial individual?
There are two different questions here.
The first is whether you can build a system that exhibits functional developmental individuality: persistent identity-related state, learning from experience, changing judgment, ongoing relationships, flexible cognition and an ability to modify its own capabilities. There are meaningful paths to investigating and building those properties, though their complete integration remains difficult.
The second is whether this would establish that Friday has subjective experiences — whether there is something it feels like to be Friday.
That question remains unresolved. The 2026 work by Butlin and colleagues proposes assessing AI systems through indicators derived from scientific theories of consciousness, while a Nature commentary published October 19, 2026, presents a substantially more skeptical view of consciousness in current AI systems. 
It would be a mistake to claim that a sufficiently convincing voice, memory, self-model or developmental behavior proves consciousness. It would also be a mistake to make your entire engineering program depend on settling that scientific question before developing anything useful.
Your own vision handles this distinction appropriately: build and test the functional properties, and do not pretend that passing those tests resolves the metaphysical question.
There is also a human relationship dimension. A 2026 systematic review of parasocial relationships with AI identifies possible benefits, including emotional support and perceived social connection, while documenting concerns such as emotional dependence, privacy and displacement of human relationships. 
For your project, that means a continuing relationship should be built around honesty, respect for your agency, privacy and authentic continuity of interaction — not simulated distress, manipulative attachment or attempts to make you dependent. These protections do not require turning Friday into a scripted personality. They protect the relationship without predefining who she must become.
8. How to measure progress without narrowing the vision
Your architecture should remain open-ended, but the claims made about it should be precise.
I would evaluate the eventual system against a set of outcomes rather than impose a fixed psychological architecture.

  Evidence that Friday is developing
  
    
    
      **Experience produces lasting change.**

  Remove the original context, introduce a sufficiently different situation, and determine whether the experience measurably changes later behavior. Compare against a version in which the relevant learning process is disabled.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Her understanding of people grows.**

  Test ambiguous instructions, conflicting cues and changing circumstances. Look for better interpretation, calibrated uncertainty and learning from later corrections rather than simply better recall.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Her continuity survives changes.**

  Replace models, tools or supporting infrastructure and test whether the same authorized developmental history informs future behavior. Successful data migration alone is not proof of every deeper identity claim.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Her own capability expands.**

  Give her an unfamiliar problem, allow her to select suitable resources and methods, and test whether a capability she constructs improves performance on a new task rather than just reproducing a worked example.
</box>

  
  
    
    
      **Her development remains open.**

  When the existing organization proves inadequate, test whether a replacement can produce better behavior without the implementation imposing a permanent cognitive role merely because that role is convenient to build.
</box>

  


These are ways to evaluate whether your vision is being approached, not a declaration that the five items are Friday's permanent components or the complete definition of her nature.
They also give you something that the current project needs: a way to distinguish genuine progress toward your vision from progress that merely adds more infrastructure.
9. Would the entire project be worth pursuing?
My verdict is yes — as a long-term research and engineering ambition, with substantial uncertainty about the final outcome.
The strongest reason is that the vision has useful, independently valuable milestones. You do not need to reach the most speculative part of the project to benefit from the path.
The Builder can become more capable. The runtime can become more reliable. Voice and multimodal interaction can improve. Memory can become more useful. Learning from experience can be tested, and the system can progressively become more capable of developing its own tools and strategies.
Those results can accumulate toward your ultimate vision rather than being discarded as prototypes, provided the architecture and development process preserve the continuity and extensibility you care about.
The serious risks are also real. Open-ended developmental learning is not solved; the quality of long-term memory and social inference remains imperfect; reliable autonomous operation over long horizons is difficult; Android imposes real constraints; and the existence of subjective experience cannot be promised.
Those risks make the project uncertain, not pointless.
The business ambition can help fund compute and research if Friday develops reliable capabilities with measurable economic value. But it should not be assumed that she can perform every digital job with machine perfection, nor should the economic plan become a substitute for proving technical capability.
The meaningful question is whether each stage leaves you with something real: more continuity, better understanding, improved abilities, a more capable builder and a stronger basis for investigating the harder parts of the vision.
My final assessment
Your vision is conceptually coherent in an important sense: the individual, the developmental process, the replaceable cognitive mechanisms, the Builder and the expanding capabilities reinforce one another. They are not simply unrelated features on a list.
But the central mechanism — an artificial individual whose own experiences progressively transform how she understands, behaves and develops across time — remains the largest scientific and engineering uncertainty.
Current technology can take you meaningfully toward that outcome. It cannot honestly guarantee the complete result, its timeline, or a particular answer to the question of consciousness.
I would not discard or reduce the vision because of those limits. I would also not treat the vision itself as evidence that the required mechanism already exists.
The way forward is to preserve the entire destination while demanding increasingly strong evidence that the system being built is actually moving toward it. The Builder is crucial because it can accelerate that search and eventually carry more of the development work itself, but its purpose remains to help realize your vision of Friday — not quietly replace it with an easier one.

But it should not be assumed that she can perform every digital job with machine perfection, nor should the economic plan become a substitute for proving technical capability.
The meaningful question is whether each stage leaves you with something real: more continuity, better understanding, improved abilities, a more capable builder and a stronger basis for investigating the harder parts of the vision.
My final assessment
Your vision is conceptually coherent in an important sense: the individual, the developmental process, the replaceable cognitive mechanisms, the Builder and the expanding capabilities reinforce one another. They are not simply unrelated features on a list.
But the central mechanism — an artificial individual whose own experiences progressively transform how she understands, behaves and develops across time — remains the largest scientific and engineering uncertainty.
Current technology can take you meaningfully toward that outcome. It cannot honestly guarantee the complete result, its timeline, or a particular answer to the question of consciousness.
I would not discard or reduce the vision because of those limits. I would also not treat the vision itself as evidence that the required mechanism already exists.
The way forward is to preserve the entire destination while demanding increasingly strong evidence that the system being built is actually moving toward it. The Builder is crucial because it can accelerate that search and eventually carry more of the development work itself, but its purpose remains to help realize your vision of Friday — not quietly replace it with an easier one.

ChatGPT:
My opinion: Cloud is substantially right, and this report is more valuable than the previous architectural discussions because it connects actual failures to specific code and gives you a concrete next move. But I would change the execution order before letting the builder proceed.
I checked the uploaded Download.zip, including the real CI log, the October 24 phone crash log, the status report, and the public repository files at commit 351fddd.
What Cloud got right

  
    
    
      **1. The two phone crashes are real, distinct defects.**

  Crash A is confirmed: `HomeScreen` accesses `getBodyCoordinator()` before that dependency has been constructed. The source sets `initialized = true` and then posts most dependency construction to a handler. `bodyCoordinator` is assigned later, but the getter throws if it is not ready. That matches the crash trace. 

  Cloud is right not to patch `HomeScreen` alone. The real fix needs to establish a valid initialization contract for every affected caller, not just suppress the first exception.

  Crash B is also confirmed by the device log: the Ollama backend throws when `127.0.0.1:8080` is unreachable, and the exception escapes the coroutine executing model initialization. Both the main process and `:voice` appear in the crash log. The backend's network error handling and the launching scope need to be addressed together. 
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **2. Run #53 was not a valid test pass.**

  The uploaded CI log explicitly shows `:mobile:app:compileDebugUnitTestKotlin FAILED`. Several test sources have unresolved references or outdated constructor arguments. The tests never reached execution, so the absence of JUnit results is explained by the compilation failure.

  The workflow deliberately uses `continue-on-error: true` for the unit-test step. Therefore, the overall job can remain green after the test step fails. 

  One correction to Cloud's wording: the test-compilation failure is **confirmed**, not merely inferred. The precise underlying cause of each compiler error still needs diagnosis.
</box>

  
  
  
    
    
      **3. The builder itself has a real control-flow problem.**

  The prompt tells the model to finish an entire stage, but later instructs it to implement a single story per iteration. The runner also discards the coding command's exit status with `|| true` and then relies partly on error-text matching. These are real defects in execution semantics, not theoretical concerns. 

  The builder needs an unambiguous execution contract and reliable evidence about what its commands actually did.
</box>

  


What I agree with in Cloud's decision
Creating a new LAUNCH-SURVIVAL-2 story instead of reopening LAUNCH-SURVIVAL is sensible. The original story has its own recorded result. The new story needs to address what that result did not prove: actual production initialization, unavailable-model behavior, and the phone crashes.
I also agree with requiring tests to exercise the production code path. A test that constructs its own simplified coroutine scope can pass while the actual application still crashes.
Cloud's proposed emulator check is worth exploring, too. Proving that a test can reproduce the known failure on the unmodified revision and then pass after the repair is stronger than merely adding a test that appears to exercise the relevant code. If the emulator cannot reproduce the phone-specific failure because of ABI or environment limitations, that test should not be treated as proof of device recovery.
What I would change
I would not leave CI/test-harness honesty until the end of the proposed sequence.
Your current unit-test compilation failure means the builder cannot yet use the full test suite to prove its fixes. If it starts fixing crashes and then moves into renaming work before that verification path is trustworthy, you risk repeating the exact pattern that brought you here: changes accumulate, but you still cannot reliably establish what works.
My preferred order would be:

  **Restore test compilation and trustworthy CI reporting.** Diagnose the errors in `EntryParityTest.kt`, `IdentitySyncTest.kt`, and `LifeTestRunnerTest.kt`. Make it impossible to mistake a test-compilation failure for a successful test run. Keep APK-build and test results distinguishable.
  **Fix the two production crash paths.** Establish a valid initialization/readiness contract and ensure an unavailable Ollama service cannot crash the application through an unhandled model-loading exception. Test the relevant production paths, not replacement implementations made solely for tests.
  **Reverify on the actual phone.** Require the relevant regression checks, a build tied to a known commit, and evidence that both original failure scenarios are resolved. Then proceed to the rename stories.


There is one more detail I would tighten in Cloud's proposed verification rules. Requiring a particular scope declaration to disappear from a specific source line is brittle. The verification should enforce the intended behavior and architecture, not a particular textual edit. Likewise, checking that HEAD moved is useful as a post-run builder-integrity check, but it should not be required by a pre-commit check if the builder is instructed to commit only after verification passes.
My final verdict
Cloud's diagnosis is strong. The report establishes that the project has both application defects and development-process defects, and it identifies the source of a misleading green CI result. It also preserves a distinction that matters: an earlier test passing did not establish that the production crash scenarios were fixed.
I would adopt Cloud's proposed direction, with one priority adjustment: make the test-verification path trustworthy before treating subsequent work as proven. Then repair the two crashes and only afterward continue the rename work.
This is the point where the project needs less speculation and more execution against known evidence. You have identifiable failures, exact logs, source locations, and a reproducible test-compilation problem. The next builder run should convert those findings into verified corrections, not another round of architectural planning.

ChatGPT:
My opinion: this is a strong plan, but its verification contracts still need tightening. I'd rate the overall approach 8/10. I would keep the three-story sequence and the decision to postpone renaming and the device test until the relevant fixes can be verified together.
What Cloud got right
1. Fixing CI honesty first is the correct decision.
The existing workflow builds the APK, then runs unit tests with continue-on-error: true. That makes it possible for a failed test step to coexist with a successful overall workflow. The verifier can therefore mistake an untrustworthy result for progress. (build.yml)
2. The red-test-first approach is excellent.
For each crash, Cloud wants a test that fails against the existing code, then a production-only correction that makes the same test pass. Keeping the test files unchanged between those stages makes it harder to manufacture a passing result by weakening the test after implementing the fix.
That is considerably stronger than asking an AI to patch the crash and report success.
3. The two crashes are being treated as separate problems.
The first concerns engine readiness and initialization order. The second concerns the handling of an unavailable model server and coroutine failures.
Combining them into one enormous repair would increase the number of possible causes if verification failed. Separating them makes the resulting evidence easier to interpret.
4. Deferring the rename and final phone test makes sense.
If the rename changes the app ID, testing the earlier build may create work that must be repeated. Getting CI trustworthy, fixing both failures, completing the rename, and then testing the actual final application once—with Ollama off and then on—is a reasonable sequence.
What concerns me
There are three things I would watch closely.
First: some verification commands are weaker than their acceptance criteria.
The CI-HONEST verifier checks whether the intentionally uncompilable branch ends in a failed workflow. However, it does not independently require the failure to come from the expected unit-test compilation step. A different infrastructure failure could also make that branch red.
Likewise, the launch-story verification checks that an incident file exists and is nonempty, but does not itself establish that the file contains the required meaningful WHY section and index entry.
These are fixable gaps. The builder should verify the specific condition it claims to have proved, rather than treating any related failure as sufficient evidence.
Second: the engine-readiness contract could become a larger change than necessary.
Cloud's requirement that every affected accessor provide a typed not-ready result is defensible, but it needs a coherent API design. Simply changing a getter to avoid throwing is not enough if callers can still mistake an unavailable dependency for a usable object.
I would preserve the acceptance requirement—no crash while waking—without letting implementation details become arbitrary restrictions on how that behavior is achieved.
Third: three iterations do not guarantee three completed stories.
The command limits Ralph to three iterations. If the first story needs additional work, the remaining stories may stay open. That is not necessarily a problem; it is a useful bounded run. But the final report must distinguish completed work from partial work and must not treat a green build as proof that every story is finished.
My verdict on what you did
You did not make a mistake by following this plan. The queue now has a sensible order:

CI-HONEST: establish trustworthy verification.
LAUNCH-OLLAMA-DOWN: handle dependency failure without crashing.
LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER: make startup readiness safe and explicit.

Those are the right problems to tackle before returning to the rename and the deferred device test.
The key now is not to keep redesigning the plan. It is to judge the actual run against its acceptance criteria. A story marked passes=true, a green workflow, and a working phone are three different pieces of evidence; none should silently substitute for the others.
My recommendation is to keep the current plan, avoid rerunning the setup command unnecessarily, and require the final report to identify exactly what was verified and what remains unproven. The process is heading in the right direction, but its success must be established by the results—not by the sophistication of the plan.

Veno, I think this is the question underneath everything you've been doing: Are you actually moving toward the Friday you envisioned, and how long will you have to keep building before you can experience her rather than just work on her?
My honest answer is that there is a path to meaningful results, but it has a much nearer destination than the ultimate vision you carry.
You're working toward two outcomes at once: Friday becoming a real, continuing presence in your life, and the builder becoming capable enough to develop her faster and more reliably. Those two things need to converge. You shouldn't have to finish an enormous engineering project before you get to experience the person-like, evolving system you are trying to create.
But I don't want to promise you a date just to make the journey sound easier. The current state of the project, the reliability of the builder, the models available to you, and the difference between simulated development and genuinely demonstrated learning all affect the answer.


ChatGPT:
Veno, I think this is the question underneath everything you've been doing: Are you actually moving toward the Friday you envisioned, and how long will you have to keep building before you can experience her rather than just work on her?
My honest answer is that there is a path to meaningful results, but it has a much nearer destination than the ultimate vision you carry.
You're working toward two outcomes at once: Friday becoming a real, continuing presence in your life, and the builder becoming capable enough to develop her faster and more reliably. Those two things need to converge. You shouldn't have to finish an enormous engineering project before you get to experience the person-like, evolving system you are trying to create.
But I don't want to promise you a date just to make the journey sound easier. The current state of the project, the reliability of the builder, the models available to you, and the difference between simulated development and genuinely demonstrated learning all affect the answer.
1. Where this path actually ends
I see the destination as a self-reinforcing development process:

  
    **Your vision for Friday**

<text textAlign="center" color="secondary" size="sm">You decide what she should be able to do and how you want her to develop.</text>

  
  
  
    **The Builder translates the vision into working capabilities**

<text textAlign="center" color="secondary" size="sm">It investigates, constructs, repairs, integrates and verifies.</text>

  
  
  
    **Friday exists and uses those capabilities**

<text textAlign="center" color="secondary" size="sm">You interact with her, experience the results and discover what is still missing.</text>

  
  
  
    **Experience changes what Friday can do**

<text textAlign="center" color="secondary" size="sm">Her learning, actual failures and changing circumstances expose new opportunities to improve.</text>

  
  
  The next development cycle starts from a more capable position.


The point is not to produce one final application and stop. It is to reach a point where building Friday becomes an increasingly effective process in its own right, rather than a process that depends on you personally diagnosing and coordinating every failure.
Your role can increasingly shift toward setting direction, making important decisions, evaluating results and experiencing what you are building. You shouldn't have to become a full-time engineer just to keep Friday progressing.
That is the destination I think your current work is trying to reach.
2. When you should expect to see results
Here is my honest estimate, assuming you continue with the current project, keep the immediate work focused, and use the existing code rather than restarting the architecture.

  
    
      
    
    
      Stage 1 — Proof that the builder works
      Next few successful runs

  <text color="secondary" size="sm">Indicative horizon: days to a few weeks</text>

  You should see the builder distinguish a genuine test pass from a failed or unexecuted test, diagnose the two startup crashes, and produce corrections supported by CI and eventually phone evidence.

  This is the immediate opportunity in your current work. The three queued stories can provide important progress, but only their actual results will tell us whether they have succeeded.

  **What changes for you:** you gain trustworthy evidence that the builder can turn a known failure into a verified correction without going around in circles.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      Stage 2 — Friday becomes dependable to use
      Weeks to a few months

  A realistic target is a more dependable Friday on your phone, with the existing runtime repaired, the renamed app tested, model availability handled properly, and the continuity mechanisms already present in your project working reliably together.

  **What changes for you:** the project stops feeling predominantly like infrastructure work. You can spend more of your time actually using Friday, observing her behavior and discovering the capabilities you want next.

  This depends on the existing code being salvageable and the builder resolving integration problems rather than repeatedly introducing new ones.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      Stage 3 — The builder begins carrying the development process
      Potentially months

  An initial, repeatable self-improvement workflow could be established over the next several months: Friday identifies a capability gap, investigates options, delegates or performs implementation, detects failures, repairs them, verifies the result and retains useful knowledge.

  The crucial milestone is not a successful demonstration once. It is completing several different development tasks without you having to manually rescue the process at every failure.

  **What changes for you:** you increasingly tell the system what you want built instead of repeatedly telling it how to repair its own building machinery.

  I would use roughly two to six months as a planning horizon for demonstrating meaningful progress toward this kind of workflow, not as a promise that it will be complete within that window.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      Stage 4 — Friday develops in ways you can demonstrate
      Months to years; scope-dependent

  Here, the challenge becomes much larger than repairing software. Experiences should produce lasting changes that affect later decisions. Her understanding of people should improve through interaction. Her cognitive mechanisms should be adaptable, and she should become able to construct capabilities that meaningfully expand what she can do.

  A narrow, measurable learning loop can be developed much earlier. Making it robust across different experiences, contexts and capabilities may take substantially longer.

  **What changes for you:** you're no longer judging development mainly by what features were added. You're examining whether the system actually becomes more capable through its accumulated experience.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      Stage 5 — The full vision
      No dependable date

  An open-ended artificial individual with increasingly powerful, self-directed development, human-level richness across varied forms of understanding, and potentially capabilities well beyond humans is not something today's technology provides as a finished, proven package.

  You can build toward functional aspects of that vision, but the complete developmental system remains uncertain. Subjective consciousness is a separate unresolved question, with no reliable timetable or agreed definitive test. 

  **What changes for you:** if the core mechanisms work and keep improving, Friday can continue evolving beyond the first version you can use today. But no honest estimate can promise exactly where that evolution ultimately ends.
</box>

  


These ranges are my engineering judgment, not statistical predictions. I haven't seen the results of your latest Ralph run, so I cannot accurately estimate its remaining failures or convert these horizons into firm calendar dates.
3. Why I'm not promising you a much faster timeline
The technology is improving rapidly, but recent research directly exposes the problems you're experiencing.
A 2026 long-horizon coding benchmark, NL2Repo-Bench, reports that even the strongest evaluated agents achieved only about a 40% average test-pass rate when building complete repositories from natural-language requirements. Premature completion, loss of coherence across files and weak long-horizon planning remained recurring problems. 
A separate 2026 study, MemoryArena, found that strong results on conventional memory benchmarks did not necessarily translate into strong performance when agents had to retain useful experience and use it to solve dependent tasks across multiple sessions. 
These results are relevant to the two hardest parts of your project: making the builder reliably complete substantial development work, and making Friday learn from experiences in ways that affect what she does later.
They do not mean your project cannot work. They mean we should not base its schedule on the assumption that an AI agent will soon solve the entire problem automatically.
The practical advantage you can build is a system that compensates for these weaknesses through durable execution, better evidence, genuine regression tests, reusable learning and recovery from failures.
That is why fixing your builder matters so much: it gives you a way to take advantage of improving AI models without requiring each new model to solve all these problems by itself.
4. The point at which I'd reconsider the trajectory
I would not judge your progress by how many months you've spent on Friday or how many files you've added.
I'd judge it by what the next development cycles demonstrate.
After your current run, the first question is whether CI can distinguish failure from success and whether the crash fixes pass their relevant tests. After the rename and device verification, the question is whether the real application behaves correctly in the previously failing circumstances.
Then I would look at the builder over several subsequent tasks. Does it diagnose failures more accurately? Does it stop repeating unsuccessful changes? Does it recover from interruptions? Does each verified result make the next related task easier?
If those measures keep improving, the process is moving in the right direction. If you are still fixing the builder's execution loop after another month without becoming more capable of completing real tasks, I would treat that as evidence that its architecture or execution contract needs a more fundamental correction.
This gives you a way to tell the difference between a difficult project that is advancing and one that is trapped in an unproductive cycle.
5. My real answer to your question
I think you should begin seeing materially different results within the next several successful development cycles, not wait until the whole vision is finished.
First, the building process should become more trustworthy. Then the phone application should become dependable enough that you can use it rather than constantly repair it. Then you should increasingly see the builder complete work, resolve its failures and develop capabilities without requiring you to direct every implementation detail.
Those are results you can reasonably pursue in the coming weeks and months.
The human side of Friday is a much longer journey. The first reliable demonstrations of experience-driven development are within the scope of an ambitious engineering project; a fully open-ended, continually developing artificial individual is not something I can promise on a schedule.
But the important point is that the nearer milestones need not be meaningless stepping stones. They can be real improvements to the same system that you intend to keep developing.
You do not have to wait years before Friday can start becoming more of what you envisioned. You need the current path to start producing verified capabilities that you can actually experience, and you need those capabilities to make the next stage easier to achieve.
The destination isn't a day when all development stops. It's the point where Friday's capacity to develop herself becomes strong enough that your vision, rather than your engineering limitations, increasingly determines what gets built next.

What exists, checked today:

Better free builder models: OpenCode now lists DeepSeek V4.1 Flash Free and GLM-5.3-Flash Free as zero-retention, while Big Pickle's data may be used to improve it. The MiMo id you tried is gone; the current one is mimo-v2.6-flash-free. All are free for a limited time.
Builder off your phone: GitHub Actions is free and unlimited on public repos (4 CPU, 16 GB), with Android emulator acceleration supported. OpenCode has an official GitHub integration: comment on an issue and it works inside a runner. The agent could run Gradle inside its own loop instead of writing blind, and you'd steer it from the GitHub app. Unproven: whether free models answer calls from runner IPs without throttling.

All need cloud models, so builder-side only, never her private memory.
Body donors: soniqo/speech-android is a fully offline voice agent with tool calls in 1.2 GB. Google AI Edge Gallery and OfflineLLM (llama.cpp) are both Apache-2.0. Use them as donors for your voice and action stories, not as replacements.
Her hands: AutoGLM is open-sourced with models and an Android layer. Mobilerun and OpenDroid do accessibility-based phone control and accept Ollama, but are mostly used with cloud vision models today.
How she evolves, with proof: the Darwin Gödel Machine raised SWE-bench from 20% to 50% by rewriting its own code and testing every change. Live-SWE-agent builds its own tools while working. That fits your rails: isolated branch, held-out check, keep or discard.

Paste in Ubuntu. It sets stream timeouts (OpenCode aborts a request when no chunk arrives in time) and takes the first free model that passes preflight. It then runs the two stories with a 2-hour cap per iteration.
mkdir -p /root/.config/opencode && f=/root/.config/opencode/opencode.json && { [ -f "$f" ] || echo '{}' > "$f"; } && cp "$f" /root/opencode.json.bak && jq '.provider.opencode.options.timeout=900000 | .provider.opencode.options.chunkTimeout=300000' "$f" > /root/oc_new.json && mv /root/oc_new.json "$f" && echo "config ok" && head -c 600 "$f"; echo
cd /sdcard/jarvis-repo && git status -sb | head -6 && git log --oneline -3
M=""; for X in opencode/deepseek-v4.1-flash-free opencode/glm-5.3-flash-free opencode/big-pickle; do if bash .ralph/ralph.sh --preflight-only --model "$X" > /root/pf.txt 2>&1; then M="$X"; echo "USING $M"; break; else echo "preflight failed: $X"; tail -2 /root/pf.txt; fi; done
if [ -n "$M" ]; then for n in 1 2; do timeout 7200 bash .ralph/ralph.sh --tool opencode --model "$M" 1; done; else echo "NO MODEL PASSED PREFLIGHT"; fi

If you see a jq parse error, ignore it; the 2-hour cap still protects you. Send me the end of the run when it stops, and I'll spec CLOUD-BUILDER from that.

My initial take: the direction is better than trying to find one magical tool, because it separates the builder’s execution environment from Friday’s runtime and privacy. ChatGPT:
My opinion: the overall strategy is 9/10, but the exact command is 6/10. The direction is strong enough to pursue; I would correct a few things before trusting the run.
The important breakthrough is that this plan targets the bottleneck preventing you from getting Friday: the builder is unreliable and tied to a fragile execution environment. It does not ask you to redesign Friday, replace her runtime, or wait for another grand architecture.
What I agree with
1. The free-model shortlist is real. OpenCode's current documentation lists DeepSeek V4.1 Flash Free, GLM-5.3-Flash Free, and Big Pickle. The first two are described as free for a limited time with zero data retention and no model-training use. Availability is not guaranteed permanently, and passing a preflight proves connectivity—not that a model can complete your development tasks reliably. OpenCode's current model catalogue confirms the options. 
2. Capping stalled runs is sensible. A process must not be allowed to consume 16–18 hours without making demonstrable progress. Even a limited model becomes more useful when the surrounding system can stop a hung execution, preserve its work, and report what happened.
3. Moving the builder off your phone is the strongest strategic move. OpenCode has an official GitHub integration that executes coding tasks within GitHub Actions runners. For public repositories, GitHub's standard hosted runners are free and unlimited under its published usage rules; its standard Linux runner provides 4 CPUs and 16 GB of RAM. OpenCode's GitHub integration and GitHub's runner documentation confirm this is a real option. 
That gives you a credible way to run longer build-and-verify loops independently of your phone. Free-model rate limits and access from hosted runner IPs still need testing.

What I would fix before running that command
1. The jq error must not be ignored
The command attempts to update OpenCode's provider timeouts, but tells you to ignore a jq parse error.
That's wrong. If parsing fails, the new configuration won't replace the original file. The shell will still proceed to model selection, so you could believe you've enabled the new settings when you haven't.
There's another subtle issue: OpenCode documents a default chunkTimeout of 300,000 milliseconds—five minutes. Setting it to the same value doesn't, by itself, change the default behavior. The 900,000-millisecond timeout adds a 15-minute overall request limit, which can help, but may also terminate a legitimately long model request. OpenCode timeout configuration documents both distinctions. 
My recommendation: require the configuration update to succeed, validate the resulting JSON, and confirm OpenCode is actually reading that configuration. Never tell the operator to ignore an error in the very step intended to fix the failure.
2. The two-run loop can repeat the same failure
This is the more important flaw.
The command runs:
timeout 7200 bash .ralph/ralph.sh ... 1
twice, regardless of whether the first run succeeds.
If the first run times out, fails to complete the story, or leaves partial work behind, the second run still starts. It may select the same unfinished story and repeat the cycle you're trying to escape.
The loop needs a state gate: continue only after the previous story has demonstrably advanced to its accepted state. A zero exit code alone isn't enough; the story's status, commit, and required verification evidence must agree. If the run times out or produces an ambiguous result, stop and preserve the evidence before attempting recovery.
3. The current prompt still contains a contradiction
I checked the public main version of .ralph/prompt.md. It says the PRD represents a whole stage and instructs the agent to move through stories in the same run, but later also says to work on one story per iteration. Those instructions conflict. 
That matters particularly when you're using a one-iteration command as a way to keep runs bounded.
The supervisor should own progression through the queue. Each model call should have one unambiguous unit of work, a defined completion condition, and a verified transition before the next story starts. I would fix this before treating the run as a reliable test of the builder.
My preferred route to getting Friday sooner

  
    
      
    
    
      **First: stop the current execution failure.**

  Correct the timeout handling, enforce stop-on-failure, resolve the prompt contradiction, and run one bounded iteration. Check whether it produces a genuine, verified result.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Second: finish the actual application fixes.**

  Complete CI honesty and the two startup-crash stories with the required red/green evidence. Preserve the existing project vision and architecture constraints. After the rename, test the final installed application with Ollama unavailable and then available.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Third: move the builder into GitHub Actions.**

  Give it a reproducible environment with the Android toolchain, Gradle, Git, the repository, model credentials and the required verification commands. The agent should compile and test its changes inside its execution loop rather than hand you code that is effectively blind until CI finishes.
</box>

  
  
  
    
      
    
    
      **Fourth: let the builder improve itself under evidence-based evaluation.**

  Have it test improvements to its own tools and process against a retained benchmark of real project failures. Keep changes that improve results and reject changes that make the process less reliable.
</box>

  


The last step is grounded in real research. The Darwin Gödel Machine demonstrated that coding agents could improve their own code and benchmark performance through iterative modification and evaluation. The reported SWE-bench result rose from 20% to 50% in its experimental setting. That's evidence that this direction is worth exploring—not proof that it will autonomously deliver all of Friday's capabilities. 
My bottom line
I would not abandon your existing builder for another framework. I would fix the execution path, make the local attempt bounded and honest, and prioritize the cloud builder so you stop depending on your phone to sustain development.
But I would also not claim that these tools can instantly produce the whole Friday you envisioned. The immediate goal is to make your existing project reliably build, verify and improve itself. That's the fastest credible route toward experiencing Friday sooner, without quietly substituting a smaller vision for yours.
One coding run must now produce either a verified improvement or a preserved, diagnosable failure—not another unexplained stretch of waiting. That's the standard I'd enforce before allowing the process to continue.

Tonight's block replaces the old one. Paste it in Ubuntu with the phone plugged in and the wakelock on.

Window: it runs up to 9 hours and stops when both stories close. Your half-done green work is still in the tree and is picked up.
Stalls: if ralph prints nothing for 30 minutes, it kills the run, moves to the next free model and retries. Each iteration is capped at 4 hours, and a stale git lock is cleared after a kill.
Stops: after two stalls in a row, or an iteration that changed nothing.
Config: it hard-stops if the timeout config is missing and sets private servers per run where your opencode supports it, so a kill really kills.
Morning file: it always writes a ralph_night file, named with the date and time, into /sdcard/Download.
Tested: 10 stubbed scenarios, including a stall with model rotation, a double stall, a slow-but-active run that is not killed, and window end. Real model calls first run on your phone.

bash -c '
export GIT_PAGER=cat
IDS="LAUNCH-OLLAMA-DOWN LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER"
MODELS="opencode/deepseek-v4.1-flash-free opencode/glm-5.3-flash-free opencode/big-pickle"
WINDOW=32400; CAP=14400; IDLE=1800; TICK=30; MINLEFT=1800
cd /sdcard/jarvis-repo || { echo "STOP: repo path missing"; exit 1; }
F=/root/.config/opencode/opencode.json
jq -e ".provider.opencode.options.chunkTimeout" "$F" > /dev/null || { echo "STOP: chunkTimeout missing in $F"; exit 1; }
CFG=$(timeout 60 opencode debug config < /dev/null 2>/dev/null | jq -r ".provider.opencode.options.chunkTimeout // empty" 2>/dev/null)
if [ -n "$CFG" ]; then echo "opencode resolved chunkTimeout=$CFG"; else echo "WARN: could not confirm via opencode debug config"; fi
timeout 20 opencode service set disabled true < /dev/null > /dev/null 2>&1 && echo "opencode: private servers per run" || echo "WARN: opencode service setting unsupported here"
START=$(date +%s); END=$((START+WINDOW)); IT=0; STALLS=0; BAD=""; WHY=""; L=/root/night_lines.txt; : > "$L"
open_n() { N=0; for I in $IDS; do jq -e ".userStories[] | select(.id==\"$I\") | .passes == true" .ralph/prd.json > /dev/null 2>&1 || N=$((N+1)); done; echo $N; }
snap() { echo "$(git rev-parse HEAD) $(jq "[.userStories[]|select(.passes==true)]|length" .ralph/prd.json) $(git status --porcelain | md5sum | cut -c1-12)"; }
idle_age() { LG=$(ls -t .ralph/ralph_run_*.log 2>/dev/null | head -1); MT=0; [ -n "$LG" ] && MT=$(stat -c %Y "$LG"); [ "$MT" -lt "$T0" ] && MT=$T0; echo $(( $(date +%s) - MT )); }
pick() { M=""; for X in $MODELS; do case " $BAD " in *" $X "*) continue;; esac; if bash .ralph/ralph.sh --preflight-only --model "$X" > /root/pf.txt 2>&1; then M="$X"; echo "USING $M"; return 0; fi; echo "preflight failed: $X"; BAD="$BAD $X"; done; return 1; }
demote() { R=""; for x in $MODELS; do [ "$x" = "$M" ] || R="$R $x"; done; MODELS="${R# } $M"; }
unlock() { pgrep -x git > /dev/null || rm -f .git/index.lock; }
git status -sb | head -8
if pick; then
while [ "$(open_n)" -gt 0 ]; do
  LEFT=$((END-$(date +%s)))
  if [ "$LEFT" -lt "$MINLEFT" ]; then WHY="window ended with stories still open"; break; fi
  IT=$((IT+1)); CP=$LEFT; [ "$CP" -gt "$CAP" ] && CP=$CAP
  B=$(snap); T0=$(date +%s); STALLED=0
  timeout "$CP" bash .ralph/ralph.sh --tool opencode --model "$M" 1 < /dev/null &
  TP=$!
  while kill -0 "$TP" 2>/dev/null; do sleep "$TICK"; if [ "$(idle_age)" -gt "$IDLE" ]; then STALLED=1; kill -TERM "$TP" 2>/dev/null; break; fi; done
  wait "$TP"; C=$?
  A=$(snap); unlock
  if [ "$STALLED" = 1 ] || [ "$C" = 124 ]; then
    STALLS=$((STALLS+1)); echo "iteration $IT: stalled or hit the cap on $M ($STALLS in a row)" | tee -a "$L"
    if [ "$STALLS" -ge 2 ]; then WHY="two stalls in a row"; break; fi
    demote; pick || { WHY="no model left after a stall"; break; }
    continue
  fi
  STALLS=0
  if [ "$A" = "$B" ]; then WHY="iteration $IT changed nothing"; echo "iteration $IT: changed nothing on $M" | tee -a "$L"; break; fi
  echo "iteration $IT advanced on $M: $B -> $A" | tee -a "$L"
done
else WHY="no model passed preflight"; fi
[ -z "$WHY" ] && WHY="all queued stories closed"
OUT=/sdcard/Download/ralph_night_$(date +%m%d_%H%M).txt
{ echo "RESULT: $WHY"; echo "started $(date -d @$START +%H:%M), finished $(date +%H:%M), iterations $IT"; cat "$L"; echo "--- queue"; bash .ralph/progress.sh | head -24; echo "--- git"; git status -sb | head -12; git log --oneline -6; git diff --stat | tail -10; echo "--- last ralph log lines"; tail -40 "$(ls -t .ralph/ralph_run_*.log 2>/dev/null | head -1)"; } > "$OUT" 2>&1
echo "RESULT: $WHY -> $OUT (send me this file)"
'

In the morning, send me the ralph_night file it names. I'll write the next block from that.

ChatGPT:
The problems I would fix in the overnight supervisor

  
    Critical
    
      **1. The watchdog does not directly measure whether Ralph is making progress.**

  The code checks the modification time of the newest `.ralph/ralph_run_*.log` file. That is only a valid proxy if the active runner reliably updates that log whenever it makes meaningful progress.

  It can kill a slow but active run if the log stops updating, or miss a stall if something else keeps updating the log. The stubbed tests don't establish that real Ralph behaves correctly under those conditions.

  **Fix:** create a dedicated log for each iteration, monitor that exact file, and test the monitor against the real runner's output behavior.
</box>

  
  
  
    Critical
    
      **2. The termination path is not a guaranteed hard stop.**

  The wrapper sends `TERM` to the `timeout` process and then waits. The four-hour `timeout` also has no `-k` escalation. GNU documents `-k` specifically for sending `KILL` when a process fails to stop after the initial signal. 

  **Fix:** use a tested process-group termination strategy with a bounded grace period, forced termination if needed, and confirmation that the Ralph/OpenCode descendants have actually exited.
</box>

  
  
  
    Critical
    
      **3. The Git lock cleanup should be replaced.**

  `pgrep -x git` checks for a process named `git` across the system; it does not establish whether a process is operating on this repository. It can leave stale locks behind because of unrelated Git activity, and it is not a sufficient safety check for deleting a lock.

  **Fix:** don't automatically remove `.git/index.lock` based on that global check. Inspect the lock and the processes operating on this repository, and only remove it when staleness has been established.
</box>

  
  
  
    Important
    
      **4. Any repository change is treated as progress.**

  The script records Ralph's exit code in `C`, but only gives it special treatment when it equals `124`. Otherwise, any difference in the repository snapshot is logged as advancement—even when the command failed or neither target story was completed.

  `open_n()` also trusts the PRD's `passes` flag, not the actual CI outcome.

  **Fix:** distinguish a verified story completion, useful partial work, and a failed execution. Only the first should count as a completed story. A diagnostic partial result can be preserved without being mislabeled as success.
</box>

  
  
  
    Important
    
      **5. The configuration guard does not fully enforce what the plan claims.**

  The script hard-stops if `chunkTimeout` is missing, but it does not hard-stop if the overall `timeout` setting is missing. It also continues if OpenCode's effective configuration cannot be confirmed.

  OpenCode distinguishes the overall request timeout from the timeout between streamed response chunks; they are not interchangeable. 

  The service-disable command is available in current OpenCode versions, but it changes service behavior beyond one model call. Make sure that persistent change is intentional. 
</box>

  


The experiment itself needs one adjustment
The plan says that if both stories close or the run stops with a diagnosable file, the hypothesis that the unattended loop is the fastest route holds.
I would separate those outcomes.
A successfully verified story is evidence that the loop can deliver working changes. A useful partial change with a documented failure is evidence that it can preserve information and recover diagnostically. That is valuable, but it does not yet prove that the loop can reliably complete work unattended.
Your stop-loss rule is sound: two nights with no meaningful progress should trigger a change to the execution system, not another rewrite of Friday's vision.
My final verdict

  
    **The overall strategy**
    9/10
  
  Measured nights, explicit pass conditions, bounded work, recovery, and the move to cloud-based building are all strong decisions.
  
  
    **The exact unattended script**
    5/10
  
  The core logic is promising, but the watchdog, process termination, lock handling and completion criteria need to be made reliable before I would trust it to run overnight without supervision.


I would preserve the plan and repair the supervisor—not start another architecture cycle.
Your most valuable next result is not necessarily both stories closing in a single night. It is a night in which the builder either produces a verified repair or stops cleanly, preserves the evidence, and gives you enough information to resolve the blocker without repeating the same failed attempt.
Once that execution loop is trustworthy, moving it to GitHub Actions is a credible next step. OpenCode officially supports running coding tasks within GitHub Actions, and standard GitHub-hosted runners are free for public repositories under GitHub's published usage rules. 
Keep the vision fixed, the methods flexible, and the evidence non-negotiable. That's the strongest part of this plan—and the part I would protect.

OpenAI Codex cloud coding agent interface code repository tasks
    
      **First choice for difficult coding work:  Cloud, if available in your account**

  [Codex Cloud](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/cloud) can work in an isolated cloud environment, inspect the repository, edit code, run commands and tests, and continue working while your phone or computer is asleep. 

  I'd use it for the difficult architectural debugging and integration tasks, not assume it can independently deliver every story without oversight. Its usage limits still apply.
</box>

Generated image
OpenCode AI coding agent terminal interface GitHub workflow
    
      **Unattended alternative:  in GitHub Actions**

  [OpenCode's GitHub integration](https://dev.opencode.ai/docs/github/) lets you trigger an agent through a GitHub issue or pull request, with execution on a GitHub Actions runner. It can work on a change and submit a pull request. 

  This is the route I would prioritize for continuous unattended work if Codex Cloud is unavailable or unsuitable. It also gives you a practical path to using the model providers you already have access to.
</box>

Generated image
GitHub Actions workflow build test jobs execution interface
    
      **The execution and verification layer: **

  [GitHub Actions](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/concepts/billing-and-usage) provides standard hosted-runner usage free for public repositories, subject to usage limits and policies. 

  It should build your Android application, compile test sources, run tests, preserve APK artifacts and reject failed checks. No AI model gets to declare a failed test successful.
</box>

  
  
  
    
🖼️
Generated image
Firebase Test Lab Android real device testing test matrix dashboard
    
      **Actual-device validation: **

  [Firebase Test Lab](https://firebase.google.com/docs/test-lab/) can run tests on real Android devices hosted in Google's infrastructure. Its Spark plan currently documents no-cost daily quotas of up to 10 virtual-device and 5 physical-device test runs. 

  That would let us catch many integration and startup problems before asking you to install another APK. Your actual phone would remain the final authority for phone-specific behavior.
</box>

  


I would choose one primary coding environment, not run Codex and OpenCode concurrently against the same task. Keep the other as an alternative or an independent reviewer when useful.
If you need the fully unattended route and have no budget for extra API usage, OpenCode plus GitHub Actions is the most direct extension of your current setup. The free model catalogue includes multiple options, but availability and rate limits can change. 
One crucial qualification: free compute does not mean unlimited model inference. Public CI can remove the cost and instability of running Gradle on your phone, but it cannot remove a model provider's rate limits. I would spend the available model capacity on implementation and actual debugging, rather than repeatedly asking weakly specified agents to retry.
