What v1.2 changes:

Each lane is tested in its own private log, with title requests off.
It reads NVIDIA's live model list and tests up to 10 coding models directly. Each result records the HTTP code, time and reply, and only models that answer become lanes.
If nothing works, the run ends red in about 50 minutes instead of 4h20m green, with the table on the run page.
Optional MISTRAL_API_KEY, GROQ_API_KEY or OPENROUTER_API_KEY secrets are picked up automatically.
The block first runs a 3-minute lane test and prints the table on your screen. It starts the long run only if a lane answered.

ChatGPT:
Mate, I agree. Stop running these increasingly elaborate installer blocks. We need to stop experimenting with the setup and establish one dependable construction system.
First, an important distinction about the latest proposal:
The v1.2 block is not the cloud builder. It is a model-availability test followed by an attempt to launch the builder. Its fake-provider tests establish that the script handles simulated responses; they do not establish that NVIDIA works with your actual key, that a coding agent can complete a real task, or that a verified Friday APK will be produced.
We've been testing the machinery around the machinery.
I would change that now.
My decision: build one cloud-native, checkpointed Builder, with a real coding agent, real Android builds, independent verification and a bounded model budget. Keep your phone out of the construction process.
1. The exact setup I would use now
My chosen setup



Layer
Tool
Decision



Source of truth

OpenAI Codex GitHub Action

Reporting
GitHub workflow summaries

Why this particular combination?
GitHub's standard public-repository Linux runner is free and provides 4 CPUs and 16 GB RAM. That is enough to run the coding-agent client and Android build together. The actual model runs remotely, so we aren't trying to load a large coding model into those 16 GB. 
The important choice is Codex.
OpenAI maintains an official GitHub Action, openai/codex-action@v1, specifically for running the coding agent in GitHub workflows. It supports repository modification, sandbox permissions and output capture. 
I would stop using the rotating OpenCode free-model arrangement as the primary construction mechanism.
Not because OpenCode is inherently bad, but because your development process has repeatedly been blocked by inconsistent free-provider availability.
2. The actual workflow
This is the system I would implement.

  
    **Existing Friday vision + PRD**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">VISION.md · .ralph/prd.json · source · tests</text>

  
  
  
    **1. Task selection**

Select exactly one unfinished story, establish its dependencies and acceptance conditions.

  
  
  
    **2. Codex construction worker**

Reads the vision and relevant architecture, investigates the code, implements the story and produces a candidate patch.

<text color="secondary" size="xs">No permission to write to main. No unrestricted GitHub credentials.</text>

  
  
  
    **3. Candidate branch**

The patch becomes a separate branch and pull request. The construction worker does not control promotion.

  
  
  
    **4. Independent verification**

The actual Gradle build, unit tests, story verification, regression tests and artifact checks run without the model API key.

  
  
  
    **5. Promotion**

Successful changes become eligible for approval and integration. Failed changes stay isolated.

  
  
  
    **Verified Friday APK**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Artifact tied to the exact verified commit</text>

  


There are three separate permission zones:



Job
Permissions



Coding worker
Repository read, isolated workspace write


Verifier
Repository read, no model secret


Publisher
Narrow GitHub write permissions, no model secret


Codex's official security guidance supports this separation: keep the API key out of the job environment, use the protected Codex action, and separate patch generation from the job that opens a pull request. Its :workspace permission profile is the starting point for workspace edits; dependencies must be prepared before that step because this profile does not grant network access. 
One more important GitHub detail: a push made with GITHUB_TOKEN normally will not trigger another push-based workflow. The workflow must explicitly dispatch the verification workflow or use an appropriately scoped GitHub App token. 
That prevents the kind of silent CI orchestration failure already encountered in your cloud-builder proposal.
3. How much autonomy?
I would configure it differently from the previous nine-hour runs.



Setting
My initial decision

1


Agent workers
1

1 per task

45 minutes initially


Automatic identical retries
0


Provider retry
At most 1 bounded retry

Direct main modification
Never


Release replacement
Only after successful new delivery


Why just one worker?
Because we need to prove that the builder can complete a real task. Parallelism without a functioning integration process only multiplies failures.
Once the first series of changes is consistently verified, I would increase construction capacity:

First, multiple independent stories on separate branches.
Then multiple connected capabilities with shared integration contracts.
Then multiple developmental programs operating concurrently.

At that point I would introduce Temporal as the durable orchestration backbone for jobs that span workflows, workers and infrastructure.
I would not install Kubernetes, Temporal, a distributed event bus and several databases before the basic cloud builder works.
The construction system should become more sophisticated because its workload demands it, not because its architecture looks impressive.

The cloud builder is only half the system. This is the runtime I would actually start with.

These are implementation donors and replaceable mechanisms, not permanent definitions of her cognitive organization.
I would not add a fixed MemoryEngine or ReasoningEngine and decide that those organs must forever own those capabilities.
The human side remains liquid. The capability fabric allows different mechanisms to develop and combine according to her needs.

The acquisition process is:
Find → Compare → Understand → Investigate → Construct → Test → Integrate → Evaluate → Improve.
REA is used when behaviour is opaque, not for every algorithm we discover.
The Builder keeps the entire vision as its context while changing the technical route when evidence reveals a better one.

6. The financial reality
I need to be particularly direct about this.

  **Cloud computation**

  Free for your public repository's standard hosted runners
  Reliable coding-agent inference
  Requires a dependable model-access arrangement
  Standard GitHub Actions compute being free does not make model inference free.
  The Codex Action uses an API key in its standard public-repository CI setup. Do not assume that your ChatGPT subscription automatically pays for those API calls. The advanced ChatGPT-account authentication path is documented separately and is not intended for public/open-source repository workflows.
My choice would be one API provider with a deliberately restricted development budget, one task per run, and usage monitoring.
If zero spending is an absolute condition, we can keep a free-provider adapter. But I will not call that dependable. Your own reports have already shown what happens when a free provider stops accepting requests.
The builder must stop when its model is unavailable—not spend another night waiting.
7. What I would do with your actual project
This is the part I don't want to get wrong again.
According to your latest report:

The queue was at 10/16 completed.
LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER remained unfinished.
No new main commit or friday-latest release was produced.
The v1.2 provider tests were simulated; the real NVIDIA API remained untested.

Therefore, I would not run v1.2 and then another nine-hour supervisor.
I would replace the unstable model-lane orchestration with the actual coding-agent workflow.

  
    **Install the cloud construction workflow**

Normal version-controlled YAML and prompt files. No massive base64 installers, no phone-hosted development loop.

  
  
    **Make LAUNCH-WAKE-ORDER the first real construction task**

It is the known unfinished launch story. Preserve its existing acceptance criteria.

  
  
    **Run the actual coding agent**

Not a one-word model test. It must inspect the repository, make the story's actual changes and submit a candidate patch.

  
  
    **Run the real Android build and verification**

Use the project's existing Gradle module. Based on the earlier project evidence, the relevant application module is `mobile:app`; the current checkout must confirm the exact task names.

  
  
    **Deliver a verified APK**

First retain it as a build artifact. Do not delete the last working release to make room for an unverified replacement.

  
  
    **You install and test Friday**

Check the actual phone behaviour. Cloud verification cannot replace this.

  


After that, the Builder moves to the next capability work already represented by your project vision and backlog.

Tested:

The lint is clean except one false alarm: my linter doesn't know the action's permission-profile input, but the action's v1 tag has it.
I ran each step's real script against a copy of your actual repo, with a local stand-in for GitHub:
story pick and red-branch merge

Not tested:

To start (no commands):

In the repo, tap Add file → Create new file. Name it .github/workflows/construct.yml, paste the file below, and commit.
Go to Settings → Secrets and variables → Actions and add a secret named OPENAI_API_KEY.
Optional: in Settings → Actions → General, allow Actions to create pull requests. Otherwise you get a compare link instead.
Go to Actions → construct → Run workflow.
When it finishes green, open the pull request. Download the APK from the CI run linked in it, test it on the phone, then merge.

Leave the old ralph.yml alone; it isn't used.
name: construct

# One real construction task per run, in four separate permission zones.
#   worker    the only job that sees the model key. Read-only token. Produces a patch, nothing else.
#   candidate applies the patch, pushes a candidate branch, starts the real Android CI on it. No model key.
#   verify    runs the story's own acceptance command (from .ralph/prd.json) against that CI run. Read-only. No model key.
#   promote   only after verify passes: marks the story, opens a pull request. Never touches main.
# Nothing here retries by itself and nothing here waits for a model to come back.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      story:
        description: "Story id from .ralph/prd.json. Empty = the open story with the lowest priority number."
        required: false
        default: ""
      note:
        description: "Optional extra instruction for the worker, for example the failure evidence of the last run."
        required: false
        default: ""
      publish:
        description: "false = stop after the worker and only keep its patch (a cheap first look). true = the full run."
        type: boolean
        default: true

concurrency:
  group: construct
  cancel-in-progress: false

permissions:
  contents: read

jobs:
  worker:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 75
    outputs:
      story: ${{ steps.pick.outputs.story }}
      title: ${{ steps.pick.outputs.title }}
      red: ${{ steps.pick.outputs.red }}
      base: ${{ steps.pick.outputs.base }}
    steps:
      - name: Checkout
        uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          fetch-depth: 0
          persist-credentials: false

      - name: Pick the story and build the work base
        id: pick
        env:
          IN_STORY: ${{ inputs.story }}
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          ID="$IN_STORY"
          if [ -z "$ID" ]; then
            ID=$(jq -r '[.userStories[] | select(.passes != true)] | sort_by(.priority) | .[0].id // empty' .ralph/prd.json)
          fi
          [ -n "$ID" ] || { echo "No open story in .ralph/prd.json"; exit 1; }
          jq -e --arg id "$ID" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id)' .ralph/prd.json > /dev/null || { echo "Unknown story $ID"; exit 1; }
          [ "$(jq -r --arg id "$ID" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | .passes' .ralph/prd.json)" != "true" ] || { echo "Story $ID is already closed"; exit 1; }
          TITLE=$(jq -r --arg id "$ID" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | .title' .ralph/prd.json)
          RED=$(jq -r --arg id "$ID" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | .verifyCommand' .ralph/prd.json | grep -oE 'refs/heads/audit/[A-Za-z0-9._/-]+' | sed 's#^refs/heads/##' | sort -u | head -1 || true)
          BASE=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
          git config user.name "friday-construct"
          git config user.email "friday-construct@users.noreply.github.com"
          if [ -n "$RED" ]; then
            git fetch --no-tags origin "+refs/heads/$RED:refs/remotes/origin/$RED"
            git merge --no-ff -m "construct: red tests ($RED) under the green work" "origin/$RED"
          fi
          {
            echo "story=$ID"
            echo "title=$TITLE"
            echo "red=$RED"
            echo "base=$BASE"
          } >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          jq --arg id "$ID" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | del(.notes)' .ralph/prd.json > "$RUNNER_TEMP/story.json"
          echo "Story $ID: $TITLE (red branch: ${RED:-none})" >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

      - name: Write the construction contract
        env:
          IN_NOTE: ${{ inputs.note }}
          RED: ${{ steps.pick.outputs.red }}
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          mkdir -p .construct
          echo ".construct/" >> .git/info/exclude
          cat > .construct/prompt.md <<'EOF'
          You are the construction worker for the Friday project. Read AGENTS.md first, then .ralph/VISION.md.
          Build exactly ONE story in this checkout: the story below, nothing else.

          How this run is organised (this differs from .ralph/prompt.md, which was written for the old phone loop):
          - You only edit files in the working tree. Do NOT run git commit, git push or git checkout, and do not create branches.
          - You have no network and must not run Gradle. CI builds and tests the result after you finish (see AGENTS.md).
          - Do NOT edit .github/**, .ralph/prd.json, .ralph/ci_verify.sh, .ralph/ralph.sh or .ralph/cloud/**. The run fails if you do.
          - Do not mark the story as passing. A separate job does that after CI and the story's own acceptance command pass.
          - From .ralph/prompt.md use only the discipline sections: plan first; grep the real sources before every import and
            record fixes in .ralph/SYMBOL_FACTS.md; systematic debugging. Ignore its steps about committing, branches, prd.json and progress.txt.
          - If the story has a RED-first criterion and a red branch is named below, that criterion is already done: its test files are in
            the tree and must stay byte-identical. Build the remaining criteria only.
          - Create the files the acceptance criteria name, at the paths they name. Put the mechanisms-considered notes in the incident file
            under a heading "Mechanisms considered".

          Finish with a final message of at most 30 lines: the files you changed, how each acceptance criterion is met or why it is not,
          and what remains unproven. Do not claim anything works: CI decides.
          EOF
          {
            echo
            echo "## Story (from .ralph/prd.json)"
            cat "$RUNNER_TEMP/story.json"
            echo
            echo "## Red branch already merged under this work: ${RED:-none}"
            if [ -n "$IN_NOTE" ]; then
              echo
              echo "## Extra instruction from the operator"
              printf '%s\n' "$IN_NOTE"
            fi
          } >> .construct/prompt.md

      - name: Run Codex
        id: codex
        uses: openai/codex-action@v1
        timeout-minutes: 50
        with:
          openai-api-key: ${{ secrets.OPENAI_API_KEY }}
          permission-profile: ":workspace"
          prompt-file: .construct/prompt.md
          output-file: .construct/final-message.md
          model: ${{ vars.CODEX_MODEL }}
          effort: ${{ vars.CODEX_EFFORT }}

      - name: Capture the candidate patch
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          git add -A
          mkdir -p cand
          git diff --cached --binary > cand/candidate.patch
          [ -s cand/candidate.patch ] || { echo "Codex changed nothing, so there is nothing to verify"; exit 1; }
          cp .construct/final-message.md cand/final-message.md 2>/dev/null || echo "(no final message)" > cand/final-message.md
          {
            echo "### Worker finished"
            git diff --cached --stat | tail -40
          } >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

      - name: Keep the patch
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: candidate
          path: cand/
          if-no-files-found: error

  candidate:
    needs: worker
    if: ${{ inputs.publish }}
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 20
    permissions:
      contents: write
      actions: write
    outputs:
      branch: ${{ steps.push.outputs.branch }}
    steps:
      - name: Checkout the exact base the worker used
        uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          ref: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.base }}
          fetch-depth: 0

      - name: Fetch the patch
        uses: actions/download-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: candidate
          path: cand

      - name: Build the candidate branch, push it and start the real CI on it
        id: push
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}
          STORY: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.story }}
          RED: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.red }}
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          git config user.name "friday-construct"
          git config user.email "friday-construct@users.noreply.github.com"
          BR="candidate/${STORY}-${GITHUB_RUN_ID}"
          git checkout -q -b "$BR"
          if [ -n "$RED" ]; then
            git fetch --no-tags origin "+refs/heads/$RED:refs/remotes/origin/$RED"
            git merge --no-ff -m "construct: red tests ($RED) under the green work" "origin/$RED"
          fi
          git apply --index cand/candidate.patch
          BAD=$(git diff --cached --name-only | grep -E '^(\.github/|\.ralph/(ci_verify\.sh|prd\.json|ralph\.sh|cloud/))' || true)
          if [ -n "$BAD" ]; then
            echo "The worker changed protected files, so this candidate is refused:"
            echo "$BAD"
            exit 1
          fi
          git commit -q -m "construct: ${STORY} (candidate, not yet verified)"
          git push origin "$BR"
          echo "branch=$BR" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)
          gh workflow run build.yml --ref "$BR"
          N=0
          for _ in $(seq 1 36); do
            N=$(gh run list --workflow build.yml --branch "$BR" --json headSha --jq "[.[] | select(.headSha == \"$SHA\")] | length")
            [ "$N" -ge 1 ] && break
            sleep 10
          done
          [ "$N" -ge 1 ] || { echo "The CI run for $BR did not start within 6 minutes"; exit 1; }
          echo "Candidate branch $BR at ${SHA:0:10}; the real Android CI is running on it." >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

  verify:
    needs: [worker, candidate]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 100
    permissions:
      contents: read
      actions: read
    steps:
      - name: Checkout the candidate
        uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          ref: ${{ needs.candidate.outputs.branch }}
          fetch-depth: 0

      - name: Run the story's own acceptance command
        env:
          GITHUB_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}
          STORY: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.story }}
          BR: ${{ needs.candidate.outputs.branch }}
          BASE: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.base }}
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set +e
          git fetch -q origin '+refs/heads/audit/*:refs/heads/audit/*' || true
          git checkout -q -B "$BR" "origin/$BR"
          git show "$BASE:.ralph/prd.json" > "$RUNNER_TEMP/base_prd.json"
          CMD=$(jq -r --arg id "$STORY" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | .verifyCommand' "$RUNNER_TEMP/base_prd.json")
          if [ -z "$CMD" ] || [ "$CMD" = "null" ]; then echo "No verifyCommand for $STORY"; exit 1; fi
          sudo mkdir -p /sdcard
          sudo ln -sfn "$GITHUB_WORKSPACE" /sdcard/jarvis-repo
          export CIV_BRANCH="$BR" CIV_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=3600
          bash -c "$CMD" 2>&1 | tee "$RUNNER_TEMP/verify.log"
          RC=${PIPESTATUS[0]}
          {
            echo "### Acceptance command for $STORY: exit $RC"
            echo '```'
            tail -60 "$RUNNER_TEMP/verify.log"
            echo '```'
          } >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
          exit "$RC"

  promote:
    needs: [worker, candidate, verify]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 15
    permissions:
      contents: write
      pull-requests: write
      actions: read
    steps:
      - name: Checkout the verified candidate
        uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with:
          ref: ${{ needs.candidate.outputs.branch }}
          fetch-depth: 0

      - name: Fetch the worker's message
        uses: actions/download-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: candidate
          path: cand

      - name: Mark the story and open the pull request
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}
          STORY: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.story }}
          TITLE: ${{ needs.worker.outputs.title }}
          BR: ${{ needs.candidate.outputs.branch }}
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          git config user.name "friday-construct"
          git config user.email "friday-construct@users.noreply.github.com"
          python3 - <<'PY'
          import os
          sid = os.environ["STORY"]
          t = open(".ralph/prd.json", encoding="utf-8").read()
          i = t.index('"id": "%s"' % sid)
          j = t.index('"passes": false', i)
          k = t.find('"id":', i + 1)
          assert k == -1 or j < k, "passes flag not found inside the story"
          t = t[:j] + '"passes": true' + t[j + len('"passes": false'):]
          open(".ralph/prd.json", "w", encoding="utf-8").write(t)
          PY
          jq -e --arg id "$STORY" '.userStories[] | select(.id == $id) | .passes == true' .ralph/prd.json > /dev/null
          echo "$(date -u +%F): $STORY verified by construct run $GITHUB_RUN_ID on candidate $BR (CI green, acceptance command passed)." >> .ralph/progress.txt
          git add .ralph/prd.json .ralph/progress.txt
          git commit -q -m "construct: $STORY verified (CI green, acceptance command passed)"
          git push origin "HEAD:refs/heads/$BR"
          CI_URL=$(gh run list --workflow build.yml --branch "$BR" --limit 1 --json url --jq '.[0].url')
          {
            echo "Verified candidate for story $STORY."
            echo
            echo "- CI run with the debug APK artifact: $CI_URL"
            echo "- The story's own acceptance command passed against that run."
            echo "- Not proven by any of this: behaviour on a real phone. Install the APK and test before you trust it."
            echo
            echo "Worker's final message (unverified claims, for reading only):"
            echo
            head -c 6000 cand/final-message.md
          } > "$RUNNER_TEMP/pr.md"
          if gh pr create --base main --head "$BR" --title "$STORY: $TITLE" --body-file "$RUNNER_TEMP/pr.md"; then
            echo "Pull request opened for $BR. Merge it when you accept the change." >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
          else
            echo "Verified. Pull requests from Actions are not allowed on this repo yet, so open it yourself: https://github.com/$GITHUB_REPOSITORY/compare/main...$BR?expand=1" >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
          fi
```

Second: verification must be tied to the exact commit.
A green CI result from some earlier commit must never validate a newer candidate. The story's acceptance evidence should identify the candidate SHA, corresponding CI run and verification result.
Third: developmental capacity must grow.
Once single-story construction is dependable, the Builder should learn to coordinate multiple independent tasks, then connected capabilities, then entire developmental programs.
Don't add that complexity before it has earned it.

The Codex worker needs an OpenAI API key with prepaid API usage. Your ChatGPT subscription does not automatically provide that API budget.

If you accept a small controlled API budget, this is the route I'd choose.
If zero spending remains absolute, retain the workflow architecture but use a free provider only after proving it can complete an actual coding task with tool access.

I would not pretend that the second option has the same reliability.
How this connects to the actual Friday vision
This is the part I don't want lost.
The workflow is not the ultimate Builder. It is the first dependable construction capability.
It establishes that the system can:

Understand an objective.
Investigate the real project.
Construct a candidate.
Obtain independent evidence.
Integrate accepted changes.

Those five abilities are the beginning of the developmental workshop.
As Friday develops, these capabilities can progressively become tools she understands and uses herself. Eventually, she can direct research, construct capabilities, investigate her own limitations and participate in changing her own architecture.
The transition from supervisor to toolset is still part of the destination.

And I strongly agree with leaving the runtime and research-tool decisions open for now. Friday's future architecture should be discovered through development, not imposed by our current favourite technologies.
This is finally a plan that moves the project toward producing Friday rather than spending another cycle producing a plan for producing Friday.

Mate, I think his plan is fundamentally right. But I found something important that I would add before settling on it.
There is another potential cloud-worker route that neither of you has properly considered: GitHub Copilot CLI running in a GitHub-hosted cloud sandbox.
And it fits your setup unusually well.
I'll explain that, then give you the improved system.
1. My verdict on his plan

  
    **Stable judge, replaceable workers**
    9/10
  
  It's the right architecture. It separates the reliability of the development process from the reliability of any particular AI provider.
  
  **Worker racing**
  8/10
  Good idea, but workers need isolated environments, identical starting points and a proper selection process.
  
  **Freebuff + Jules as the worker pool**
  7/10
  Useful right now, but neither should become a permanent dependency.


The strongest decision is that the project now has two independent things:
The judge: your repository, acceptance criteria, CI, verification scripts and protection rules.
The workers: whatever coding agents happen to be available.
That distinction means losing a provider no longer means losing the development system.
I would preserve it.
2. The worker pool I would actually create
I wouldn't depend on just Freebuff and Jules. I'd establish several routes, each with a different failure profile.

  
    **Worker A — Jules**
    Expiring
  
  Use it immediately for the current launch task. Google says sessions can be continued until November 11, 2026; after that Jules becomes read-only until its December 31 shutdown. Export its settings, memories, setup scripts and session history before the deadline. 
  Backup: Freebuff or GitHub Copilot CLI.
  
  
    **Worker B — Freebuff Cloud**
    Try now
  
  It can provide an actual cloud coding session connected to the repository. Its free availability and model capacity depend on the access tier and current capacity. 
  Backup: GitHub Copilot CLI or Jules while available.
  
  
    **Worker C — GitHub Copilot CLI**
    New candidate
  
  Copilot CLI is available on GitHub Copilot Free, although agent usage is limited. It supports cloud sandboxes through `copilot --cloud`. These sessions execute in GitHub-hosted environments, can retain their state and can be steered from other Copilot clients. 
  Backup: Codespaces-hosted CLI or another independent worker.
  
  
    **Worker D — Antigravity CLI**
    Secondary
  
  Free individual access has basic weekly limits, and its CLI supports headless operation. However, Google-managed Remote Agents require Pro or Ultra. Its Google Cloud Code Editor route also requires a Cloud Billing account or eligible credits. I would not make it your default zero-budget cloud worker. 
  Backup: GitHub Copilot CLI or a manually initiated worker.
  
  
    **Worker E — Codex CLI**
    Optional
  
  One correction to the earlier discussion: current OpenAI documentation includes limited Codex access on Free plans. Codex Cloud itself remains unavailable on Free. That makes CLI an additional interactive worker, not a dependable unattended cloud service. 
  Backup: Copilot CLI, Freebuff or another available model.
  
  **Worker F — Free model APIs**

  NVIDIA and other free API endpoints remain optional workers. They are useful when available, but must never determine whether the whole construction system operates.
The GitHub Copilot opportunity deserves attention
There are two separate GitHub facilities that shouldn't be confused.



Facility
Free plan



Copilot CLI with limited agent usage
Available


Copilot Cloud coding agent that handles assigned work
Not included


Copilot CLI --cloud sandbox
Documented, metered cloud session; account eligibility and allowance need checking


The distinction matters.
GitHub documents that copilot --cloud launches an interactive session in a GitHub-hosted sandbox. It can execute commands and modify code without using your phone's computing resources. The session can be resumed and steered from other clients. It is interactive rather than a headless -p workflow. 
There's also a financial advantage: GitHub Free personal accounts currently include $12 of monthly eligible compute usage, and cloud sandbox compute and memory can draw from that allowance. After it runs out, usage is blocked if there is no payment method, or may become billable when payment is enabled. 
Your public-repository GitHub Actions runners remain independently free. 
That gives us another potential route:
GitHub Copilot Free + GitHub-hosted cloud sandbox + existing Friday CI.
I would test its actual availability on your account before depending on it, but I would absolutely add it to the worker pool.

The GitHub Copilot opportunity deserves attention

3. Fixing the problems in the current cloud plan
Here's where I'd strengthen it.



Problem
Primary solution
Backup



Provider quota exhausted
Switch to next eligible worker
Preserve task until quota resets


Provider unavailable
One bounded retry, then switch
Another provider


Unsupported region
Check live account entitlements
Copilot CLI or another eligible service


Identity verification demanded
Decline if uncomfortable
Use a different worker


Worker cannot select the red branch
Stop before editing
Jules or Copilot CLI with explicit base branch


Worker cannot push to GitHub
Export its patch
Apply through another isolated workspace


Worker ignores instructions
Reject its candidate
Send a smaller, more constrained task to another worker


Agent freezes
Preserve partial work and terminate session
Resume or hand off to another worker

Diagnose the actual failed check
One repair attempt, then another worker


CI doesn't start
Manually dispatch build.yml
Correct branch naming


Two workers conflict
Separate candidate branches
Select one candidate and discard the other


Story is too large
Split into independently verifiable slices
Keep the original acceptance criteria as the final gate


GitHub compute quota exhausted
Use another eligible worker environment
Stop safely until allowance resets


Previous release is damaged
Keep the previous release immutable
Restore from the last verified artifact


There is a bigger improvement behind this table:
Every failure should have a classification, a recovery action and a stopping condition.
Don't just say "retry."
For example:

HTTP 429: quota or rate limit. Don't repeat immediately.
Authentication failure: check credentials. Don't rotate models blindly.
Tool failure: inspect tool output and permissions.
No agent activity: stop the session after its defined inactivity threshold.
Compilation failure: give the worker the actual compiler output.
CI infrastructure failure: retry CI, not the code-writing agent.
Story acceptance failure: identify the unmet requirement. Never mark the story complete.

This prevents another four-hour run where the supervisor keeps doing something that cannot succeed.
4. Freebuff needs a security decision
I wouldn't ignore this one.
I checked its newly published Terms and Privacy Policy.
They have an effective date of November 12, 2026. The replacement Terms explicitly require a human to initiate each free session and remain actively present while it runs. They prohibit using scripts, macros, headless browsers or external autonomous agents to operate Freebuff. Its own product automation after you start a session is allowed. 
This means Freebuff is suitable for supervised cloud construction, not your permanent unattended overnight infrastructure under those terms.
There are other details worth knowing:

Connecting a repository authorizes a limited Codebase Evaluation, which may involve automated analysis and human review by restricted partners. Those partners are bound by confidentiality and may not train models on the repository through that evaluation. 
Freebuff can derive advertising interests from project data and prompts. It says it doesn't give separately uploaded or connected repository files directly to advertising providers. 
A model or feature explicitly labelled as usable for AI training can have different data-retention and sharing conditions.
It may require government ID and a selfie for identity verification through Didit.

My recommendation: connect only the Friday repository, keep secrets out of its reachable environment, inspect the model's data-use notice and refuse identity verification if you are uncomfortable with it.
Since the current README says availability depends on tier and capacity, we also shouldn't assume which free allowance an account in Egypt receives. The live model picker decides that. 
5. Improve the worker racing mechanism
I like racing workers, but I would change how it works.
The mistake would be allowing several agents to edit the same branch or allowing whichever one finishes first to become the winner.
Instead:

  
    **Immutable task packet**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Story ID · base commit · red-test commit · acceptance criteria · permitted scope</text>

  
  
  
    
      
        **Jules**
        audit/story-jules
      
    
    
      
        **Freebuff**
        audit/story-freebuff
      
    
    
      
        **Copilot CLI**
        audit/story-copilot
      
    
  
  
  
    **Same independent judge**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Android CI · story verification · scope checks · red-test integrity</text>

  
  
  
    **Candidate selection**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Correctness, minimal changes, maintainability and evidence</text>

  
  
  
    **One winning candidate**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Human approval and integration</text>

  


I would enforce six rules:

Every worker starts from the exact same base commit.
Every worker gets a separate branch.
Every branch uses audit/ so your existing CI trigger works.
The red test file must remain byte-identical.
The same original acceptance command must be applied to each candidate.
The winner is selected by correctness and quality, not speed.

For Jules, its official documentation supports choosing the starting branch when creating a task. 
For Freebuff, branch selection and branch pushing are still unverified. Make those explicit conditions before allowing it to modify the launch story.
If it cannot start from the red baseline, it is not an appropriate worker for this particular task.
6. Add a proper failure-recovery mechanism
I would create a simple standard for every construction attempt.

  **Worker attempt record**




Field
Required information



Task
Story ID


Starting point
Exact base SHA


Worker
Jules, Freebuff, Copilot, etc.


Candidate
Branch and commit SHA


Result
Completed, stalled, rejected or failed


CI
Exact workflow run and result


Acceptance
Exact verification output


Failure
Categorized failure and evidence


Recovery
Next worker or next bounded action


  
  **Recovery rules**


A stalled agent does not restart indefinitely.
A failed candidate is never silently deleted.
A second worker can inspect the first worker's partial findings, but must work in a separate branch.
A failed CI check is passed to the worker with the full evidence.
A repair attempt must have a new hypothesis, not just repeat the original task.
If every worker is unavailable, the task remains queued with all state preserved.

That final rule matters.
There is no technical trick that can guarantee an AI worker will be available forever at zero cost. But there is a way to guarantee that unavailable workers don't destroy progress or waste another night.
We can make the development system dependable even when the intelligence supply is not.
7. The improved execution order
I would organize your current effort like this:

  
    **Immediately use Jules while it is still writable.**

Run the launch task from the red branch. Export Jules' service-held data before November 11. Its GitHub branches and PRs remain on GitHub after the service closes.

  
  
    **Run the Freebuff candidate under supervision.**

Confirm it can select the red branch, create the required `audit/` branch and preserve the test file. Don't allow it to touch `main`.

  
  
    **Establish GitHub Copilot CLI as the durable fallback.**

Check whether your free GitHub account allows CLI cloud sessions and whether its included compute allowance is available. This is the new route I'd prioritize investigating.

  
  
    **Keep the existing GitHub judge untouched.**

Run `build.yml`, the story's acceptance command and the necessary integrity checks against every candidate's exact SHA.

  
  
    **Choose one candidate and integrate it.**

The winner goes through your existing approval and merge process. Only then should the next story become eligible.

  
  
    **Increase concurrency gradually.**

Once we have several successful individual construction cycles, let the Builder coordinate multiple independent tasks. Don't force parallelism before the integration mechanism proves itself.

  


8. My final recommendation
I would make one architectural commitment:
Friday gets a permanent judge and a replaceable worker ecosystem.
The judge never depends on a particular AI company. The worker ecosystem can use different models, browser-based agents, cloud coding sessions, CLI agents or future tools.
Your primary chain becomes:

  
    **Primary**
    Available now
  
  Jules temporarily + Freebuff under supervision.
  
  **First long-term alternative**

  GitHub Copilot CLI cloud session, subject to confirming your Free-account allowance.    Second alternative
  GitHub Codespaces with Copilot CLI. Personal GitHub Free accounts include 120 core-hours and 15 GB storage monthly; this is cloud compute capacity, not unlimited AI usage.    Other available workers
  Antigravity CLI, Codex CLI and free model APIs, as their actual entitlements allow.    Last-resort recovery
  Keep the task queued, preserve all evidence, use a smaller supervised implementation task, and wait for an eligible worker instead of wasting another night.
One important financial distinction: GitHub Actions' standard runners for your public repository are free, but some cloud sandbox and Codespaces usage consumes a limited monthly allowance. Keep spending controls in place. 
My rating for this improved architecture: 9.5/10.
The remaining uncertainty is not architectural. It is whether the individual services actually grant your account the advertised worker capacity and can handle your real repository.
I would not add another supervisor, another bootstrap or another model-rotation script.
I'd establish the worker contract, make the judge authoritative, and let whichever workers are available compete under exactly the same rules.
That way, when Jules disappears, when Freebuff changes its limits, when NVIDIA refuses requests, or when a new coding agent appears, Friday's development process survives without being rebuilt again.

There is a bigger improvement behind this table:
Every failure should have a classification, a recovery action and a stopping condition.
Don't just say "retry."
For example:

HTTP 429: quota or rate limit. Don't repeat immediately.
Authentication failure: check credentials. Don't rotate models blindly.
Tool failure: inspect tool output and permissions.
No agent activity: stop the session after its defined inactivity threshold.
Compilation failure: give the worker the actual compiler output.
CI infrastructure failure: retry CI, not the code-writing agent.
Story acceptance failure: identify the unmet requirement. Never mark the story complete.

This prevents another four-hour run where the supervisor keeps doing something that cannot succeed.
4. Freebuff needs a security decision
I wouldn't ignore this one.
I checked its newly published Terms and Privacy Policy.
They have an effective date of November 12, 2026. The replacement Terms explicitly require a human to initiate each free session and remain actively present while it runs. They prohibit using scripts, macros, headless browsers or external autonomous agents to operate Freebuff. Its own product automation after you start a session is allowed. 
This means Freebuff is suitable for supervised cloud construction, not your permanent unattended overnight infrastructure under those terms.
There are other details worth knowing:

Connecting a repository authorizes a limited Codebase Evaluation, which may involve automated analysis and human review by restricted partners. Those partners are bound by confidentiality and may not train models on the repository through that evaluation. 
Freebuff can derive advertising interests from project data and prompts. It says it doesn't give separately uploaded or connected repository files directly to advertising providers. 
A model or feature explicitly labelled as usable for AI training can have different data-retention and sharing conditions.
It may require government ID and a selfie for identity verification through Didit.

My recommendation: connect only the Friday repository, keep secrets out of its reachable environment, inspect the model's data-use notice and refuse identity verification if you are uncomfortable with it.
Since the current README says availability depends on tier and capacity, we also shouldn't assume which free allowance an account in Egypt receives. The live model picker decides that. 
5. Improve the worker racing mechanism
I like racing workers, but I would change how it works.
The mistake would be allowing several agents to edit the same branch or allowing whichever one finishes first to become the winner.
Instead:

  
    **Immutable task packet**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Story ID · base commit · red-test commit · acceptance criteria · permitted scope</text>

  
  
  
    
      
        **Jules**
        audit/story-jules
      
    
    
      
        **Freebuff**
        audit/story-freebuff
      
    
    
      
        **Copilot CLI**
        audit/story-copilot
      
    
  
  
  
    **Same independent judge**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Android CI · story verification · scope checks · red-test integrity</text>

  
  
  
    **Candidate selection**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Correctness, minimal changes, maintainability and evidence</text>

  
  
  
    **One winning candidate**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Human approval and integration</text>

  


I would enforce six rules:

Every worker starts from the exact same base commit.
Every worker gets a separate branch.
Every branch uses audit/ so your existing CI trigger works.
The red test file must remain byte-identical.
The same original acceptance command must be applied to each candidate.
The winner is selected by correctness and quality, not speed.

For Jules, its official documentation supports choosing the starting branch when creating a task. 
For Freebuff, branch selection and branch pushing are still unverified. Make those explicit conditions before allowing it to modify the launch story.
If it cannot start from the red baseline, it is not an appropriate worker for this particular task.
6. Add a proper failure-recovery mechanism
I would create a simple standard for every construction attempt.

  **Worker attempt record**




Field
Required information



Task
Story ID


Starting point
Exact base SHA


Worker
Jules, Freebuff, Copilot, etc.


Candidate
Branch and commit SHA


Result
Completed, stalled, rejected or failed


CI
Exact workflow run and result


Acceptance
Exact verification output


Failure
Categorized failure and evidence


Recovery
Next worker or next bounded action


  
  **Recovery rules**


A stalled agent does not restart indefinitely.
A failed candidate is never silently deleted.
A second worker can inspect the first worker's partial findings, but must work in a separate branch.
A failed CI check is passed to the worker with the full evidence.
A repair attempt must have a new hypothesis, not just repeat the original task.
If every worker is unavailable, the task remains queued with all state preserved.

That final rule matters.
There is no technical trick that can guarantee an AI worker will be available forever at zero cost. But there is a way to guarantee that unavailable workers don't destroy progress or waste another night.
We can make the development system dependable even when the intelligence supply is not.
7. The improved execution order
I would organize your current effort like this:

  
    **Immediately use Jules while it is still writable.**

Run the launch task from the red branch. Export Jules' service-held data before November 11. Its GitHub branches and PRs remain on GitHub after the service closes.

  
  
    **Run the Freebuff candidate under supervision.**

Confirm it can select the red branch, create the required `audit/` branch and preserve the test file. Don't allow it to touch `main`.

  
  
    **Establish GitHub Copilot CLI as the durable fallback.**

Check whether your free GitHub account allows CLI cloud sessions and whether its included compute allowance is available. This is the new route I'd prioritize investigating.

  
  
    **Keep the existing GitHub judge untouched.**

Run `build.yml`, the story's acceptance command and the necessary integrity checks against every candidate's exact SHA.

  
  
    **Choose one candidate and integrate it.**

The winner goes through your existing approval and merge process. Only then should the next story become eligible.

  
  
    **Increase concurrency gradually.**

Once we have several successful individual construction cycles, let the Builder coordinate multiple independent tasks. Don't force parallelism before the integration mechanism proves itself.

I would make one architectural commitment:
Friday gets a permanent judge and a replaceable worker ecosystem.
The judge never depends on a particular AI company. The worker ecosystem can use different models, browser-based agents, cloud coding sessions, CLI agents or future tools.
Your primary chain becomes:

  
    **Primary**
    Available now

  Antigravity CLI, Codex CLI and free model APIs, as their actual entitlements allow.    Last-resort recovery
  Keep the task queued, preserve all evidence, use a smaller supervised implementation task, and wait for an eligible worker instead of wasting another night.
One important financial distinction: GitHub Actions' standard runners for your public repository are free, but some cloud sandbox and Codespaces usage consumes a limited monthly allowance. Keep spending controls in place.

1. Why Antigravity CLI is my primary worker
This is the most important discovery.
Google has three different things that must not be confused:



Product
Availability



Antigravity desktop application
Free individual plan with basic weekly limits


Antigravity Remote Agents
Google AI Pro/Ultra required


Antigravity CLI (agy)
Free individual access with basic weekly limits


The managed Remote Agents product is paid-only. I am specifically recommending the CLI running on a machine we control, not pretending the paid Remote Agents service is free. 
Why this CLI?
It has official support for:

Linux and ARM-based development environments.
Headless execution without a desktop UI.
Structured JSON output and streaming progress.
Resuming previous conversations.
Explicit model and reasoning-effort selection.
SSH authentication using a browser-based authorization flow.

That means your phone can authorize the remote machine once. The coding agent can subsequently execute its development task on that machine.
It also has asynchronous subagents that can work in isolated Git worktrees. The paid /teamwork and /boost features are not part of my recommendation. 
The free individual plan has basic weekly limits, and Google does not publish a fixed, guaranteed number of agentic coding hours. So I would treat it as a free intelligence supply, not unlimited capacity.
There is one more safeguard: headless execution can soft-deny a tool operation when it needs approval, potentially returning exit code zero. The Builder must inspect the actual operation results and output, not trust the process exit code. 
2. Why Oracle Cloud is the host I would choose
This is the piece that changes your situation.
You currently have no desktop computer and don't want your phone responsible for hosting a large construction system.
OCI's Always Free Ampere A1 allocation provides:

1,500 OCPU-hours per month.
9,000 GB-hours of memory per month.
Equivalent to 2 OCPU and 12 GB RAM.
Up to 200 GB of Always Free block-volume storage.

The ARM architecture is suitable for hosting the Linux-based Antigravity CLI. Importantly, we do not need to run a large language model on this machine. Inference happens through Google's service.
This machine is for the agent, repository, task execution and development coordination.
I would not make it the primary Android build machine. Your project's existing build environment is already more suitable for that.
There are three honest limitations:

Oracle usually requires a phone number and credit/debit card for account verification. It says the card isn't charged unless you upgrade, although temporary authorization holds may occur. 
Free capacity is not guaranteed in every home region.
Oracle can reclaim an Always Free instance if its CPU, network and (for A1) memory utilization remain below the specified thresholds for seven days.

The recovery is to preserve the repository and Builder task state outside the ephemeral machine. If the VM disappears, we restore it rather than rebuilding Friday.
This is a persistent-capable, zero-charge host—not an unconditional promise of permanent uptime.

Let's be precise about the most useful backups.
Backup A: Jules — use it immediately
Google's shutdown announcement says sessions can continue until November 11, 2026. After that it becomes read-only until December 31, when the service closes. Export its memories, setup scripts, session history and task information before the transition. 
It's a temporary extra worker, not something I would architect around.
Backup B: Freebuff Cloud
Use it as a supervised worker when the primary agent is exhausted.
Important: Freebuff's terms effective November 12 require a human to initiate each session and remain actively present while it runs. They prohibit externally automated operation of the product. That makes it unsuitable as an unattended permanent Builder worker. Its privacy policy also covers prompts, code, repository information and connected service data. 
Backup C: Google Cloud Shell
This is a fallback when Oracle is unavailable or you cannot create the OCI account.
Its published allowance is 50 hours per week, with a maximum 12-hour session. Non-interactive sessions terminate after 40 minutes. So it is a temporary interactive remote workbench, not a persistent unattended server. 
Backup D: Kaggle
This is the alternative when cloud model availability itself becomes the blocker.
Kaggle provides notebook sessions up to 12 hours and documented GPU access with a quota typically around 30 hours per week, subject to availability and demand. 
It can run quantized open coding models locally. That won't match the stronger hosted agent, but it creates an alternative that doesn't depend on a free inference API.
I would use it for smaller, bounded construction tasks and experiments—not claim it can autonomously complete the whole Android project.

4. What happens to GitHub?
I am deliberately not making GitHub the Builder.
I would separate three things:



Responsibility
Owner



Development coordination
Your Python Builder


Software construction
Remote Antigravity CLI worker


Build and acceptance judge
Existing project verification infrastructure


GitHub currently hosts your source and your existing Android CI. Keep it there because moving the repository would create unnecessary work.
But the Builder should communicate through ordinary Git operations and portable task records. It should not depend on GitHub Actions being its scheduler, on GitHub's coding agent being available, or on GitHub being the only possible development host.
GitHub is the current repository and build adapter.
The actual Builder lives elsewhere.
For a secondary build provider, Google Cloud Build offers 2,500 free build-minutes monthly on its default e2-standard-2 2-vCPU, 8-GB machine, subject to its stated free-tier conditions. I would benchmark your actual Android project there before treating it as a valid replacement: 8 GB might be insufficient for your native build. 
No claim that this is already a proven equivalent to your existing build pipeline.
5. I would not install OpenHands right now
I went back to your OpenHands research.
OpenHands is genuinely useful: its current Software Agent SDK provides Python, TypeScript and REST interfaces, with local or isolated Docker/Kubernetes workspaces. 
But there is a reason I wouldn't make it the immediate solution:
OpenHands does not supply unlimited intelligence.
It still needs an LLM provider or a capable self-hosted model. Its isolated workspace also needs a suitable execution environment. Installing it would add another orchestration system on top of the Python Builder you already have.
Your own research notes record the following:

Python Builder tests: 17/17 passed at that recorded point.
Full Builder handover: incomplete.
Builder self-repair: incomplete.
Builder self-research: incomplete.

That is what I would continue from.
Not throw away and rebuild.
OpenHands remains a donor for useful agent and workspace mechanisms. It doesn't have to become a second Builder.
6. How this becomes the Builder you originally intended
This part is important because I don't want to give you a setup that solves only today's launch story.
I would connect the systems in this manner:

  
    **Friday's complete vision**

<text size="xs" color="secondary">The permanent developmental destination</text>

Inspects the codebase, understands development state and identifies the next capability or limitation.

Isolated workspace, temporary dependencies, reproducible environment, experiment results and cleanup.

Does the new capability work? Does it improve Friday? Does it integrate with her existing organism?

  
  
  
    **Capability becomes part of Friday**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">The Builder records its lineage and the next developmental objective.</text>

  


This begins with one worker and one coherent task.
Then the development capacity grows:

One worker constructs one bounded task.
Multiple workers investigate independent parts.
Subagents operate in isolated worktrees.
The Builder coordinates connected capabilities.
Entire developmental programs become possible simultaneously.
Friday gains the research, construction and verification capabilities herself.

We do not need to introduce Temporal, Kubernetes, NATS, OpenHands, a second database and a dozen agents before the first real result.
Your own Builder should absorb the mechanisms it needs as it grows.

There is only one useful first proof.
Not a model comparison. Not an architecture audit that ends in another report. Not another installation simulator.
<box border={{size:1,color:"#269B77"}} radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>  First real acceptance test  Remote Builder completes one real Friday story while your phone is offline.

ChatGPT:
I found something in your list that I think is considerably more useful than another coding-agent subscription.
Orca + GitHub Codespaces + Tailscale.
Together, they provide a credible way to run coding agents on a remote cloud machine, control them from your Android phone, and leave your phone disconnected while the remote machine continues working.
And unlike Oracle, this route has an actual documented free allowance that does not require you to add a payment method before using that allowance.
I also found several valuable pieces for your existing Builder. I would not install all 103 links. I'd select the mechanisms that solve different problems and leave the rest available for later.
1. The cloud setup I'd choose instead of Oracle
Best current no-card candidate

  
    
      
      **GitHub Codespaces — remote machine**
    
    
      
        120
        Free core-hours/month
      
      
        15 GB
        Monthly storage allowance
      
      
        12h
        Maximum session lifetime
      
    
    For personal GitHub Free accounts. At two cores, 120 core-hours translates to approximately 60 actual machine-hours per month. No payment method is required to use the included allowance; access is blocked after it runs out rather than automatically charging you. 
  
  
    
      
      **Tailscale — private connection**
    
    $0 Personal plan
    Up to six users and unlimited devices. It has an official Codespaces integration, so the remote machine can join your private network. 
  
  
    
      
      **Orca — remote agent control**
    
    Its remote server runs on the cloud machine. Its Android companion lets you see worktrees, monitor sessions, respond to agent questions, start agents, inspect changes and receive notifications. 
  
  
    
      
      **Replaceable coding workers**
    
    Orca supports Antigravity, OpenCode, Pi, Gemini, GitHub Copilot CLI and other CLI agents. The worker can change without changing the remote machine or mobile interface. 
  


Why this is particularly interesting
The remote server owns the repositories, worktrees, terminals and agent sessions. The mobile phone is a controller, not the computer doing the construction. Orca explicitly supports headless Linux servers and mobile pairing over Tailscale. 
The two important limitations:

Codespaces has a default 30-minute idle timeout, configurable up to four hours. Terminal input or output resets it. There is also an absolute 12-hour lifetime, even if the machine is active. 
Orca's remote server and mobile companion are currently labelled beta. Its documentation recommends private networking, not exposing the server port publicly.

One security correction: Orca defaults many agent launches to permission-bypass or YOLO flags. I would change its agent permission setting to Manual before connecting the Friday repository. An isolated worktree is useful, but it is not permission to expose every credential or trusted file to an agent. 
My judgment: This is the first combination from the research that I would seriously test for your phone-controlled cloud development.
It is not unlimited compute. It is a real, documented remote development environment with private networking, a mobile control surface and interchangeable coding workers.

  Recommended

The first proof I would demand
I would not start by installing all of this.
I would prove this single connection:
Android phone → Orca Mobile → Tailscale → Codespaces remote server → Antigravity CLI → Friday repository.
The proof is that you can start a real agent session from your phone, disconnect the phone, and have the remote agent continue working. When you reconnect, you can see its changes, terminal history and status.
Then the same remote environment can be used for the actual Builder workflow and the next project story.
This integration is supported component-by-component by the respective documentation, but I have not run the complete combined setup on your account. Orca's remote server is beta, and the Codespaces time limits remain real. 
My conclusion: The most valuable resources you found are Orca, Archon, Graphify, Superpowers, Context7, DeerFlow, PydanticAI, llama.cpp, sherpa-onnx and REA.
The most promising cloud alternative to Oracle is Codespaces with Tailscale and Orca, backed by Daytona for disposable compute and Cloud Shell for manual recovery.
And the most important architectural decision is this: don't make any one of these products the Builder. Make your existing Builder capable of using and replacing them.
That gives Friday a development environment that can survive the loss of any individual worker, host or framework without turning every failure into another rebuild of the entire project.

1. The cloud arrangement that fits your constraints
Use GitHub Codespaces Free + Tailscale Personal + Orca Remote Server + Orca Android as the first and only cloud route.
This is not an unlimited free server. GitHub currently documents 120 free compute core-hours and 15 GB-month of storage for personal Free accounts. On a two-core machine, that is about 60 actual running hours per monthly allowance. Without a payment method, usage is blocked when the included allowance is exhausted; it does not silently become unlimited free compute. 
Codespaces also has a maximum 12-hour lifetime and an adjustable idle timeout up to four hours. Work must be committed or checkpointed so a stopped workspace is an interruption, not a disaster. 
Tailscale has a documented Codespaces integration. Orca documents connecting a headless remote server to a mobile device through a private Tailscale network. 
The operating rule: no keep-alive tricks, no account cycling, no public exposure of the remote agent server, and no assumption that a free host will run forever. Start work when needed, save progress to Git, stop the workspace, and resume later. That's both more robust and more compatible with the actual limits.

The important distinction: Builder decides and coordinates; workers perform coding; verification decides whether the work is acceptable. Orca is the cockpit, not another brain. Codespaces is rented temporary compute, not Friday's permanent home.

1. The cloud arrangement that fits your constraints
Use GitHub Codespaces Free + Tailscale Personal + Orca Remote Server + Orca Android as the first and only cloud route.
This is not an unlimited free server. GitHub currently documents 120 free compute core-hours and 15 GB-month of storage for personal Free accounts. On a two-core machine, that is about 60 actual running hours per monthly allowance. Without a payment method, usage is blocked when the included allowance is exhausted; it does not silently become unlimited free compute. 
Codespaces also has a maximum 12-hour lifetime and an adjustable idle timeout up to four hours. Work must be committed or checkpointed so a stopped workspace is an interruption, not a disaster. 
Tailscale has a documented Codespaces integration. Orca documents connecting a headless remote server to a mobile device through a private Tailscale network. 
The operating rule: no keep-alive tricks, no account cycling, no public exposure of the remote agent server, and no assumption that a free host will run forever. Start work when needed, save progress to Git, stop the workspace, and resume later. That's both more robust and more compatible with the actual limits.
2. Rules that keep the cloud from rejecting or undermining the setup
There are two limitations to respect.
First: compute and AI access are separate. Codespaces provides a machine, not free model inference. The coding worker still needs an account or provider that permits it to use a model. I would start with Antigravity only if your existing Google account can authenticate it without payment. Otherwise, use the one coding CLI you can already access. Do not spend another month installing six workers and discovering none has usable inference.
Second: Orca's remote server and Android companion are beta. The server must remain running for remote sessions to continue, and Orca's documentation says the credentials and tools need to be installed on the server itself, not merely on your phone. 
There is also a security setting I would not compromise on: Orca preconfigures supported agents with permission-bypass flags. Set Settings → Agents → Agent Permissions → Manual before running them. A disposable Git worktree is useful isolation, but it is not a reason to hand an agent unrestricted permission to everything. 
The phone can disconnect after starting a task. The remote process can continue while its Codespace remains alive. But it cannot run forever: the 12-hour maximum, idle timeout and monthly allowance still apply. 
3. The build sequence — in this order
I would make this one continuous sequence. Each stage has a concrete exit condition. No jumping ahead because a new framework looks exciting.

  
    **Establish the truth about the existing project**

Do not start by rewriting Builder or changing the architecture.

Inspect the actual current repository and record:
- Current commit, branch and uncommitted changes.
- What the existing tests actually pass and fail.
- The real open tasks in the current PRD.
- What the existing CI verifies.
- Which Builder functions work, partially work or do not work.

The old project snapshot reported Python Builder, Ralph, `.ralph/prd.json`, `VISION.md`, `ci_verify.sh` and GitHub CI. Those are useful starting points, but I will not pretend that historical information verifies today's repository state. <MemoryCite />

**Exit condition:** one evidence-based baseline report. No redesign and no speculative new backlog.

  
  
    **Prove the cloud connection once**

Create one Codespace from the real Friday repository. Add Tailscale, install Orca's remote server and the one selected worker, then pair the Android phone.

Run one controlled test:
1. The phone connects to the cloud host.
2. Orca can display the repository and terminal.
3. The worker can inspect the repository.
4. It makes a tiny change on a separate branch.
5. The existing verification process checks it.
6. The change is committed and survives disconnecting and reconnecting.

**Exit condition:** a complete, evidenced round trip from phone to cloud to repository and back.

If Orca's mobile pairing is the failing component, do not throw away the cloud setup and begin another architecture search. Keep Codespaces and temporarily use its own browser interface while resolving that one problem.

  
  
    **Make the existing Builder genuinely dependable**

This is the most important engineering stage.

Builder must be able to take one existing task, understand the relevant code, define its acceptance conditions, hand work to a worker, inspect the changes, run verification, recover from failure, and report exactly what happened.

The execution cycle becomes:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
    **Existing project task**
  </box>
  <box align="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></box>
  <box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
    **Builder understands and specifies the task**
  </box>
  <box align="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></box>
  <box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
    **One worker implements in an isolated worktree**
  </box>
  <box align="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></box>
  <box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
    **Tests, CI and Builder review**
  </box>
  <box align="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></box>
  <box border radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
    **Evidence, checkpoint, human review and merge**
  </box>
  <caption textAlign="center">Failure returns to the relevant repair step, not to an endless redesign.</caption>
</box>

Crucially, Builder must preserve task progress in Git and the existing project state, not rely on a cloud terminal session remembering everything.

**Exit condition:** three different existing project tasks completed through that full process, with successful verification and at least one demonstrated interruption-and-resume. Until then, one active coding task and one worker.

  
  
    **Strengthen Builder only where real failures expose a gap**

The resources you supplied become targeted improvements, not another collection of frameworks.

I would use Graphify to help map the actual repository when code relationships become difficult to understand, and Context7 when a task depends on current library or platform documentation. Borrow Archon's useful concepts—task history, approval points and traceable workflow events—only where Builder lacks them.

I would not install Archon, DeerFlow, PydanticAI, Ruflo and several other orchestration systems alongside Builder. That would create competing authorities and another integration project.

**Exit condition:** Builder can investigate, implement, verify, explain failures and resume work reliably. New infrastructure is allowed only to solve a demonstrated problem.

  
  
    **Build the Capability Foundry into that dependable process**

Now Builder's job expands beyond implementing predefined features.

Friday identifies a capability she needs. Builder investigates how to create it, develops a prototype in an isolated environment, tests it, measures its limitations and prepares it for integration. Failed experiments are recorded; successful capabilities are versioned, evaluated and made available to Friday.

This is where the ideas you previously discussed—capability creation, limitation breaking and development through experimentation—become functioning engineering processes rather than documents describing future possibilities.

**Exit condition:** Friday can receive a genuinely new capability created through this process, use it, and have its performance evaluated.

  
  
    **Build Friday herself as an individual, not merely a coding agent**

This is a separate development goal that the Builder pipeline exists to make possible.

Start with an integrated, usable Android Friday: persistent identity and continuity, meaningful memory, voice and interaction, contextual reasoning, and the ability to use capabilities appropriately.

Her software should remain adaptable. I would not force her into a permanent, rigid set of cognitive organs or mandatory thinking loops. Capabilities should be discoverable and composable according to the situation, while her identity and developmental history remain continuous.

For the on-device path, investigate `llama.cpp` for local model inference and `sherpa-onnx` for offline speech capabilities—the two were among the strongest Android-relevant resources in your list. But the actual model and runtime choices must be measured on your phone, not selected from impressive benchmark claims. 

Measure startup time, memory, latency, battery, heat and storage. When resources are constrained, Friday should adapt her computational workload rather than simply fail.

**Exit condition:** a usable Friday that maintains continuity, interacts with you and operates within measured device constraints, with cloud capabilities available when appropriate.

  
  
    **Transfer development ability into Friday**

This is important to your ultimate vision.

Builder begins as external developmental scaffolding. As Friday gains reliable research, construction, testing, evaluation and capability-management abilities, those abilities become part of Friday's own available capabilities.

Builder should not become a permanent superior that Friday can never outgrow. Its role should progressively diminish as Friday becomes capable of directing and developing more of herself. You remain the person who sets the direction and whose corrections shape that development.

**Exit condition:** Friday can independently identify a bounded capability gap, develop a candidate solution, test it, explain the result and seek the appropriate approval to integrate it.
